# Embedded surfaces with trivial extendable mapping class groups in simply connected $4$-manifolds

**arXiv ID**: 2608.01504v1
**Authors**: Weizhe Niu
**Published**: 2026-08-02
**Categories**: math.GT
**Comments**: 84 pages, 3 figures. Comments welcome
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.01504v1

## Abstract

For every $g\geq 3$, every closed, connected, oriented, simply connected smooth $4$-manifold $X$, and every knot $K\subset S^3$, we construct infinitely many pairwise topologically inequivalent smoothly embedded oriented genus-$g$ surfaces $F\subset X$ whose orientation-preserving extendable mapping class subgroups are trivial in both the topological and smooth categories and whose first Alexander modules are isomorphic to the Alexander module of $K$. In particular, there are infinitely many such surfaces with vanishing first Alexander module. The construction is supported in a $4$-ball. Although their Alexander data are prescribed independently, the surfaces are distinguished, and their mapping-class rigidity is detected, by the nonabelian centralizer structure of their exterior groups.

## Full Text

Embedded surfaces with trivial extendable mapping class groups in simply connected 4-manifolds

in simply connected44-manifoldsWeizhe NiuYau Mathematical Sciences Center, Tsinghua Universityweizheniu@mail.tsinghua.edu.cn

## Abstract.

For everyg≥3g\geq 3, every closed, connected, oriented, simply connected
smooth44-manifoldXX, and every knotK⊂S3K\subset S^{3},
we construct infinitely many pairwise topologically inequivalent
smoothly embedded oriented genus-ggsurfacesF⊂XF\subset Xwhose orientation-preserving extendable mapping class
subgroups are trivial in both the topological and smooth categories and
whose first Alexander modules are isomorphic to the Alexander module ofKK. In particular, there are infinitely
many such surfaces with vanishing first Alexander module. The
construction is supported in a44-ball. Although its Alexander data
are prescribed independently, the surfaces are distinguished and their
mapping-class rigidity is detected by the nonabelian centralizer
structure of their exterior groups.

## Key words and phrases:knotted surfaces, rim surgery, mapping class groups, extendable homeomorphisms, Alexander modules

## 1.Introduction

A smoothly embedded surface may have intrinsic symmetries that are not
induced by ambient homeomorphisms. LetXXbe a closed, connected,
oriented smooth44-manifold, and letF⊂XF\subset Xbe a smoothly
embedded, closed, connected, oriented genus-ggsurface with a fixed
orientation-preserving identificationΣg≅F\Sigma_{g}\cong F. Its smooth
and topological orientation-preserving extendable subgroups areEX+​(F)=im⁡(π0​Diff+⁡(X,F)⟶Mod⁡(Σg))E_{X}^{+}(F)=\operatorname{im}\!\left(\pi_{0}\operatorname{Diff}^{+}(X,F)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})\right)

andEX,TOP+​(F)=im⁡(π0​Homeo+⁡(X,F)⟶Mod⁡(Σg)),E_{X,\mathrm{TOP}}^{+}(F)=\operatorname{im}\!\left(\pi_{0}\operatorname{Homeo}^{+}(X,F)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})\right),

respectively. Here every ambient homeomorphism or diffeomorphism is required to
preserve both the orientation ofXXand the orientation ofFF, and we use the canonical identification of the
homeomorphism and diffeomorphism mapping class groups ofΣg\Sigma_{g}[7, Theorem B]. Since every diffeomorphism is a homeomorphism,EX+​(F)≤EX,TOP+​(F).E_{X}^{+}(F)\leq E_{X,\mathrm{TOP}}^{+}(F).

ForX=S4X=S^{4}, writeE+​(F)E^{+}(F)andETOP+​(F)E_{\mathrm{TOP}}^{+}(F).
The basic realization problem asks how small these extendable subgroups
can be. We construct, in every genus at least three, infinitely many
such embeddings inS4S^{4}, and more generally in every closed simply
connected smooth44-manifold, with trivial topological, and hence
smooth, orientation-preserving extendable subgroup and prescribed first
Alexander module.

Two oriented surfaces inXXaretopologically equivalentif an
orientation-preserving homeomorphism ofXXcarries one to the other
and preserves their orientations. They aresmoothly equivalentif such a homeomorphism can be chosen to be a diffeomorphism. Thus
topological inequivalence implies smooth inequivalence and nonisotopy.

We also record the first Alexander module. WriteEF=X∖int⁡ν​F.E_{F}=X\setminus\operatorname{int}\nu F.

WhenH1​(EF;ℤ)≅ℤH_{1}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}with positive meridian as preferred
generator, letE~F→EF\widetilde{E}_{F}\to E_{F}be the corresponding infinite
cyclic cover and set𝒜1​(F)=H1​(E~F;ℤ).\mathcal{A}_{1}(F)=H_{1}(\widetilde{E}_{F};\mathbb{Z}).

The positive meridian determines the deck transformationtt, making𝒜1​(F)\mathcal{A}_{1}(F)a leftΛ=ℤ​[t,t−1]\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]-module. For a knotK⊂S3K\subset S^{3},𝒜​(K)\mathcal{A}(K)denotes its classical Alexander module with
the same convention.

## Theorem 1.1.

For every integerg≥3g\geq 3and every classical knotK⊂S3K\subset S^{3},
there exist infinitely many pairwise topologically inequivalent smoothly
embedded, closed, connected, oriented genus-ggsurfacesFg,n⊂S4F_{g,n}\subset S^{4}

such thatETOP+​(Fg,n)=E+​(Fg,n)=1,𝒜1​(Fg,n)≅𝒜​(K)E_{\mathrm{TOP}}^{+}(F_{g,n})=E^{+}(F_{g,n})=1,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{1}(F_{g,n})\cong\mathcal{A}(K)

asΛ\Lambda-modules.

## Corollary 1.2.

LetXXbe a closed, connected, oriented, simply connected smooth44-manifold. For every integerg≥3g\geq 3and every classical knotK⊂S3K\subset S^{3}, there exist infinitely many pairwise topologically
inequivalent smoothly embedded, closed, connected, oriented genus-ggsurfacesFg,n⊂XF_{g,n}\subset X

such thatEX,TOP+​(Fg,n)=EX+​(Fg,n)=1,𝒜1​(Fg,n)≅𝒜​(K)E_{X,\mathrm{TOP}}^{+}(F_{g,n})=E_{X}^{+}(F_{g,n})=1,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{1}(F_{g,n})\cong\mathcal{A}(K)

asΛ\Lambda-modules.

TakingKKto be the unknot gives infinitely many examples with
vanishing first Alexander module. Thus this abelian invariant can be
prescribed independently of the rigidity, which is detected by
nonabelian information in the exterior group.

The question is part of the realization problem in[1, Problem 4.39]. For the standard unknotted surface inS4S^{4}, Hirose identified the extendable subgroup with the stabilizer of
the Rokhlin quadratic form, hence a large subgroup ofMod⁡(Σg)\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[8]. Related work treats surfaces
for which every mapping class extends, criteria and obstructions for
individual classes, and extensions of prescribed periodic maps[9,11,17]. Toward small subgroups,
Liu–Ni–Sun–Wang constructed locally flat knotted tori with finite
extendable subgroup[13, Corollary 5.7], while Q. Liu
constructed locally flat surfaces in every positive genus whose
extendable subgroup has finite image on first homology[12, Theorem 1.1]. The former result does not identify the
finite subgroup, and the latter does not control mapping classes acting
trivially on first homology.

Very recently, Baykur and Sunukjian constructed a genus-4040totally
geodesic surface in a closed hyperbolic44-manifold whose smooth and
topological extendable mapping class groups are both trivial[2]. Their ambient manifold is not simply connected.
In the same paper, their iterated rim-surgery constructions in simply
connected44-manifolds control the projective action of the
extendable subgroup on first homology, rather than the full extendable
subgroup. To our knowledge,Theorem1.1gives the first smoothly
embedded closed oriented surfaces of positive genus inS4S^{4}whose
full orientation-preserving extendable subgroup is trivial even in the
topological category.

The closest direct precursor is the computation in[15]for
a single ordinary untwisted rim surgery on the standard unknotted
surface. There the extendable subgroup is determined by the Rokhlin
quadratic form, the homology class of the rim curve, and a symmetry of
the knot exterior; in particular, the entire Torelli group remains
extendable. One surgery therefore detects only the homology class of
the rim curve and cannot give a trivial extendable subgroup. Here an
ordered sequence of rim surgeries retains enough nonabelian information
to recover the individual curves.

Ordinary untwisted rim surgery alongddreplaces a product annulusS1×IS^{1}\times IbyS1×J∘S^{1}\times J^{\circ}, whereJ∘⊂B3J^{\circ}\subset B^{3}is a knot with a small open arc removed, without
changing the ambient44-manifold[4]. We use it
first to make the surface group visible in the exterior and then to
record curves determining every mapping class.

Write the standard unknotted genus-ggsurface as∂(P×I)\partial(P\times I). Annulus replacements along a complete system
of meridian-disk boundaries make the two horizontal copies ofπ1​(P)\pi_{1}(P)visible, and further surgeries along the vertical boundary
separate their images. The resultingF0⊂S4F_{0}\subset S^{4}has injective
push-offρ0:π1​(Σg,p)⟶π1​(S4∖int⁡ν​F0,∗).\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(S^{4}\setminus\operatorname{int}\nu F_{0},*).

This injectivity converts exterior-group information back into surface-
group information; seeSections4and5.

Choose an ordered collection of nonseparating curves𝒟=(d1,…,dN)\mathcal{D}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{N})

that fillsΣg\Sigma_{g}, has trivial label-preserving stabilizer inMod⁡(Σg)\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}), and satisfiesi​(di,di+1)=1(1≤i<N).i(d_{i},d_{i+1})=1\qquad(1\leq i<N).

The labels are the positions in the tuple. Such a collection is built
inTheorem12.9; successive surgeries along its curves
use hyperbolic knots with pairwise nonisomorphic meridian-marked groups.

Alexander control is independent of rigidity. Mayer–Vietoris for the
meridional infinite cyclic cover makes each local knot module a direct
summand (Section15.3). Friedl supplies a hyperbolic knot
with the prescribed module, and Kalfagianni supplies infinitely many
Alexander-trivial hyperbolic knots with pairwise nonisomorphic groups[5,10]. Use the prescribed-module knot at one preliminary stage and the
Alexander-trivial knots at all remaining stages; varying one rank-two factor
changes an intrinsically recognized knot group, hence the surface, while
leaving the module fixed. All modifications lie in a44-ball, which
also givesSection1.

Organization.Sections2–5establish normalized

surface subgroup. Sections6–11recognize and remove surgeries by Bass–Serre and centralizer methods.
Sections12–14construct the ordered
filling system and prove rigidity. Section15computes
the Alexander module, chooses the knots, proves the main results, and
records group and exterior homology.

## 2.The push-off homomorphism and extendability

The rigidity argument compares a mapping class on the surface group with
an ambient homeomorphism on the exterior group. As based maps they have
two natural ambiguities: changing the exterior basepoint gives a common
inner conjugation, and changing the positive normal-circle section gives
a meridian shear. Under the injectivity and homological hypotheses
proved later, both disappear, yielding the exact intertwining relation
used in the reverse induction.

Fixg≥3g\geq 3, a closed oriented surfaceΣg\Sigma_{g}, andp∈Σgp\in\Sigma_{g}, and writeπ=π1​(Σg,p).\pi=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p).

They determinef∗​(γ)=λf​f¯​(γ)​λf−1.f_{*}(\gamma)=\lambda_{f}\,\bar{f}(\gamma)\,\lambda_{f}^{-1}.

Changingλf\lambda_{f}changes this representative of the induced outer
automorphism by an inner automorphism; whenever an equation usesf∗f_{*}, one representative is fixed.

Aparametrized surfaceis an oriented embeddingΣg⟶S4\Sigma_{g}\longrightarrow S^{4}

with a fixed identification of its image withΣg\Sigma_{g}. For such anFF, the class[F]=0∈H2​(S4;ℤ)[F]=0\in H_{2}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z}), so its self-intersection
and the Euler class of its oriented normal22-plane bundle vanish;
the normal bundle is trivial.

Choose a sectionσ:Σg⟶∂ν​F\sigma:\Sigma_{g}\longrightarrow\partial\nu F

of the positively oriented normal circle bundle, and take the exterior
basepoint to be∗=σ(p).*=\sigma(p).WriteEF=S4∖int⁡ν​F,GF=π1​(EF,∗),E_{F}=S^{4}\setminus\operatorname{int}\nu F,\qquad G_{F}=\pi_{1}(E_{F},*),

and letι:∂ν​F↪EF\iota:\partial\nu F\hookrightarrow E_{F}

be the inclusion. Thepush-off homomorphismisρF=(ι∘σ)∗:π⟶GF.\rho_{F}=(\iota\circ\sigma)_{*}:\pi\longrightarrow G_{F}.

Letμ∈GF\mu\in G_{F}be the positive meridian, namely the positively oriented boundary of a
normal disk toFF. The chosen section identifies the image of the
boundary group with⟨ρF​(π),μ⟩,\langle\rho_{F}(\pi),\mu\rangle,

andμ\mucommutes with every element ofρF​(π)\rho_{F}(\pi).

Withσ\sigmaand∗=σ(p)*=\sigma(p)fixed,ρF\rho_{F}is a based
homomorphism. For another positive sectionσ′\sigma^{\prime}, with∗′=σ′(p)*^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}(p), choose a boundary path from∗*to∗′*^{\prime}.
Relative to∂ν​F≅Σg×S1,\partial\nu F\cong\Sigma_{g}\times S^{1},

the sections differ by a mapa:Σg⟶S1.a:\Sigma_{g}\longrightarrow S^{1}.

The induced homomorphismη=a∗:π⟶ℤ\eta=a_{*}:\pi\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

records the additional normal-circle winding. After identifying the
basepoints along the chosen path, the new push-off isρF′​(γ)=q​ρF​(γ)​μη​(γ)​q−1(γ∈π),\rho_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma)=q\,\rho_{F}(\gamma)\,\mu^{\eta(\gamma)}q^{-1}\qquad(\gamma\in\pi),

whereq∈im⁡(π1​(∂ν​F,∗)⟶GF)=⟨ρF​(π),μ⟩.q\in\operatorname{im}\!\left(\pi_{1}(\partial\nu F,*)\longrightarrow G_{F}\right)=\langle\rho_{F}(\pi),\mu\rangle.

Thusη\etais the meridian shear and the single common conjugatorqqrecords the boundary change of basepoint. Another boundary path
changesqqwithin the same boundary subgroup. The following
definition quotients by exactly these choices.

## Definition 2.1(Marked peripheral data).

With the domain of the push-off homomorphism identified withπ\piby the parametrization ofFF, let[ρF]∂[\rho_{F}]_{\partial}denote the collection of homomorphismsρ′:π→GF\rho^{\prime}:\pi\to G_{F}of the formρ′​(γ)=q​ρF​(γ)​μη​(γ)​q−1,q∈⟨ρF​(π),μ⟩,η∈Hom⁡(π,ℤ).\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)=q\,\rho_{F}(\gamma)\,\mu^{\eta(\gamma)}q^{-1},\qquad q\in\langle\rho_{F}(\pi),\mu\rangle,\quad\eta\in\operatorname{Hom}(\pi,\mathbb{Z}).

The triple(GF,μ,[ρF]∂)(G_{F},\mu,[\rho_{F}]_{\partial})

is themarked peripheral dataof the parametrized surface.
The fixed domainπ\pirecords the parametrization, the elementμ\murecords the positive meridian, and[ρF]∂[\rho_{F}]_{\partial}records the push-off homomorphism independently
of the choices of section and boundary basepoint path.

An exterior homeomorphism determines only an outer isomorphism of
exterior groups. A path in the target boundary from its basepoint to
the image of the source basepoint selects a representative; the next
proposition records its action on the push-off.

## Proposition 2.2(Naturality of the push-off homomorphism).

LetF,F′⊂S4F,F^{\prime}\subset S^{4}be smoothly embedded, closed, oriented
surfaces, and letΨ:(S4,F)⟶(S4,F′)\Psi:(S^{4},F)\longrightarrow(S^{4},F^{\prime})

be an orientation-preserving homeomorphism whose restriction to the
oriented surface inducesf∈Mod⁡(Σg)f\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}). Choose the positive
meridiansμ\muandμ′\mu^{\prime}compatibly. The induced exterior homeomorphism determines an
outer isomorphismGF⟶GF′.G_{F}\longrightarrow G_{F^{\prime}}.

After choosing a change-of-basepoint path in∂ν​F′\partial\nu F^{\prime}, this outer isomorphism has a representativeΦ:GF⟶GF′\Phi:G_{F}\longrightarrow G_{F^{\prime}}

such thatΦ​(μ)=μ′\Phi(\mu)=\mu^{\prime}andΦ​(ρF​(γ))=q​ρF′​(f∗​(γ))​(μ′)θ​(γ)​q−1\Phi(\rho_{F}(\gamma))=q\,\rho_{F^{\prime}}(f_{*}(\gamma))\,(\mu^{\prime})^{\theta(\gamma)}q^{-1}(1)

for everyγ∈π\gamma\in\pi, whereq∈⟨ρF′​(π),μ′⟩andθ∈Hom⁡(π,ℤ).q\in\langle\rho_{F^{\prime}}(\pi),\mu^{\prime}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\theta\in\operatorname{Hom}(\pi,\mathbb{Z}).

In particular, the common conjugating element may be chosen in the
image of the target boundary group.

## Remark 2.3.

The homeomorphism of exteriors determines only an outer isomorphism of
fundamental groups. A boundary change-of-basepoint path selects a
representative, and changing that path changes the representative by
one inner automorphism. The normalization below applies specifically
to automorphisms induced by homeomorphisms of pairs. It does not
assert that every automorphism ofGFG_{F}preservesρF​(π)\rho_{F}(\pi).

For the groups used later,Section5.4givesH1​(G;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩,ρ​(π)⊂[G,G].H_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle,\qquad\rho(\pi)\subset[G,G].(2)

Together with injectivity ofρ\rho, these conditions eliminate the
shear by abelianization and then remove the common conjugation by an
inner automorphism represented inρ​(π)\rho(\pi).

## Corollary 2.4(Normalization of the push-off homomorphism).

Letρ:π⟶G\rho:\pi\longrightarrow G

be injective, and letμ∈G\mu\in Gcommute withρ​(π)\rho(\pi). Suppose

All exterior groups in the construction will satisfy the injectivity and
homological hypotheses above. Thus an extendable mapping class is
represented by a meridian-fixing exterior automorphism satisfying the
literal push-off intertwining equation. The next section computes its
change under one rim surgery.

## 3.The exterior group and push-off homomorphism for one rim surgery

This section computes the exterior group and push-off homomorphism after
one ordinary untwisted rim surgery. Van Kampen gives the amalgam and a
canonical retraction to the preceding group; a based groupoid calculation
records the preferred-longitude insertions at crossings in traversal order.

## 3.3.The ordered change in the push-off homomorphism

The retraction kills the preferred longitudes; the following conventions
retain their endpoint basing and traversal order.

## Convention 3.2(Groupoid conventions for crossing paths).

For every knot group inserted inSections3,4and5,

and vertical stages are writtenℓjD\ell_{j}^{D}andℓνV\ell_{\nu}^{V}.

Letγ\gammabe a based surface loop transverse todd. Let𝒪Σ\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}consist of the surface basepointppand, for
each intersection, one point immediately before and one immediately
after the crossing arc. Let𝒪=σ​(𝒪Σ)\mathcal{O}=\sigma(\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma})be their positive normal push-offs, together with the exterior basepoint∗=σ(p)*=\sigma(p). They lie outside the replacement interior and are regarded in both
exteriors. For a spaceYYcontaining𝒪\mathcal{O}, writeΠ1​(Y;𝒪)\Pi_{1}(Y;\mathcal{O})

then the producta​babmeans thataais traversed first andbbsecond. Thus a loopλ\lambdabased atvv, transported touualongaa, isa​λ​a−1a\lambda a^{-1}.

At each crossing, the old pushed-off connector is the reference
connector in the new exterior. In the coordinates ofSection3.3, travel fromu<0u<0tou>0u>0is positive and reverse travel is negative. A boundary path from the
exit endpoint to∗loc*_{\mathrm{loc}}bases the inserted local longitude
at that endpoint; for the reverse crossing, the positive reference
connector transports it to the other endpoint. These identities use only
the section-compatible product model and ordinary untwisted gluing,
require no injective old edge map, and add no meridian or externalS1S^{1}-factor power.

## Lemma 3.3(Crossing paths in local coordinates).

Choose oriented product coordinatesN=Sθ1×[−1,1]u×Dv,w2N=S^{1}_{\theta}\times[-1,1]_{u}\times D^{2}_{v,w}

A path fromu−u_{-}tou+u_{+}has positive local intersection with the

Applying the local identity to all crossings gives an ordered formula,
which records more than algebraic intersection.

## Proposition 3.4(Push-off formula in the fundamental groupoid).

Letγ\gammabe a based surface loop transverse todd, with

Letι:Π1​(X;𝒪)⟶Π1​(EF′;𝒪)\iota:\Pi_{1}(X;\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\Pi_{1}(E_{F^{\prime}};\mathcal{O})

be induced by inclusion. ThenρF′​(γ)=\displaystyle\rho_{F^{\prime}}(\gamma)={}ι​(a^0)​κ1​(b1​ℓJϵ1​b1−1)​ι​(a^1)​⋯​⋯​κn​(bn​ℓJϵn​bn−1)​ι​(a^n).\displaystyle\iota(\widehat{a}_{0})\kappa_{1}\bigl(b_{1}\ell_{J}^{\epsilon_{1}}b_{1}^{-1}\bigr)\iota(\widehat{a}_{1})\cdots\cdots\kappa_{n}\bigl(b_{n}\ell_{J}^{\epsilon_{n}}b_{n}^{-1}\bigr)\iota(\widehat{a}_{n}).(11)

For1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n, letPjP_{j}be the portion of the modified
pushed-off loop from the global basepoint∗*to the endpoint of thejj-th crossing path. ThusPj=ι​(a^0)​κ1​(b1​ℓJϵ1​b1−1)​ι​(a^1)​⋯​ι​(a^j−1)​κj.P_{j}=\iota(\widehat{a}_{0})\kappa_{1}\bigl(b_{1}\ell_{J}^{\epsilon_{1}}b_{1}^{-1}\bigr)\iota(\widehat{a}_{1})\cdots\iota(\widehat{a}_{j-1})\kappa_{j}.

is thereforePj​bj​ℓJϵj​bj−1​Pj−1.P_{j}b_{j}\ell_{J}^{\epsilon_{j}}b_{j}^{-1}P_{j}^{-1}.(12)

SincePjP_{j}contains all longitude insertions arising from the earlier

## 5.Injectivity after rim surgery along the vertical boundary

Section4identifies both horizontal surface groups with the
same subgroupU0U_{0}:ρ|π1​(P+,p):π1​(P+,p)→≅U0\rho|_{\pi_{1}(P^{+},p)}:\pi_{1}(P^{+},p)\xrightarrow{\cong}U_{0}

but their relative position in the full closed-surface image remains unknown.
We correct it by ordinary untwisted rim surgery along the cores of the
vertical boundary. In even genus the single core separates the two vertex
images; in odd genus the first does so and the second determines the HNN
stable letter. The resulting literal based formulas support an amalgam
normal-form proof in even genus and a Britton proof in odd genus.

## Lemma 5.1(Boundary words in free groups).

LetUUbe a finite-rank free group with a fixed free basis. Call
two nontrivial elementscommensurableif some nonzero powers of
them are conjugate.
- (i)

If a nontrivial cyclically reduced wordwwcontains an
oriented basis letter exactly once, thenwwis not a proper power.
- (ii)

Ifw≠1w\neq 1is not a proper power, thenCU​(w)=⟨w⟩,C_{U}(w)=\langle w\rangle,

and⟨w⟩\langle w\rangleis a maximal cyclic malnormal subgroup ofUU.
- (iii)

Ifu,v∈U∖{1}u,v\in U\setminus\{1\}anda​⟨u⟩​a−1∩⟨v⟩≠1a\langle u\rangle a^{-1}\cap\langle v\rangle\neq 1

for somea∈Ua\in U, then the maximal cyclic subgroups containinguuandvvare conjugate. In particular, ifuuandvvare not commensurable, every conjugate of⟨u⟩\langle u\ranglehas
trivial intersection with⟨v⟩\langle v\rangle.

## Proof.

Ifw=unw=u^{n}withn>1n>1, cyclic reduction makes the cyclic word forwwannn-fold repetition, so every oriented-letter multiplicity is
divisible bynn; this proves (i).

For (ii), letrrgenerate the maximal cyclic subgroup
containingww. On the Cayley-tree axis, the orientation-preserving
translation stabilizer is⟨r⟩\langle r\rangle. An axis reversal would
square to an element fixing the axis pointwise, impossible for the free,
torsion-free action. HenceCU​(w)=⟨r⟩.C_{U}(w)=\langle r\rangle.

Ifwwis not a proper power,r=wr=w. Moreover, a conjugate of⟨w⟩\langle w\ranglemeeting it nontrivially preserves the same axis,
so the conjugator lies in⟨w⟩\langle w\rangle; thus this subgroup is
maximal cyclic and malnormal.

For (iii), a nontrivial element of the intersection is a
nonzero power of botha​u​a−1aua^{-1}andvv, so their axes coincide.
The orientation-preserving translation group of that axis is the common
maximal cyclic subgroup, giving the claimed conjugacy and the stated
noncommensurable consequence.
∎

## Lemma 5.2(A subgroup normal-form criterion).

LetG=G1∗AG2G=G_{1}*_{A}G_{2}have injective edge mapsιi:A→Gi\iota_{i}:A\to G_{i}. IfC≤AC\leq A,Ui≤GiU_{i}\leq G_{i}, andUi∩ιi​(A)=ιi​(C)(i=1,2),U_{i}\cap\iota_{i}(A)=\iota_{i}(C)\qquad(i=1,2),

then, with the mapsC→UiC\to U_{i}given byιi|C\iota_{i}|_{C}, the vertex inclusions induce an injectionU1∗CU2⟶G.U_{1}*_{C}U_{2}\longrightarrow G.

## Proof.

By the intersection hypothesis, each syllable of a reduced word outsideιi​(C)\iota_{i}(C)also lies outsideιi​(A)\iota_{i}(A). The same word is
therefore reduced and nontrivial inG1∗AG2G_{1}*_{A}G_{2}.
∎

## Proposition 5.5(Injectivity of the push-off homomorphism in odd genus).

With the preceding paths and maximal-tree choice, the stable letterτ\tausatisfies the literal based formulaρ0​(τ)=ℓ2V​(ℓ1V)−1.\rho_{0}(\tau)=\ell_{2}^{V}(\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1}.(37)

Reducedness means that, whenever(ϵj,ϵj+1)=(1,−1)(\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{j+1})=(1,-1), one hasuj∉W2−u_{j}\notin W_{2}^{-}, and whenever(ϵj,ϵj+1)=(−1,1)(\epsilon_{j},\epsilon_{j+1})=(-1,1), one hasuj∉W2+u_{j}\notin W_{2}^{+}.

Substituteτ=ℓ2V​(ℓ1V)−1\tau=\ell_{2}^{V}(\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1}and view the result inL=⟨L1,ℓ2V|[ℓ2V,w2]=1⟩.L=\left\langle L_{1},\ell_{2}^{V}\ \middle|\ [\ell_{2}^{V},w_{2}]=1\right\rangle.

A subwordτ​u​τ−1\tau u\tau^{-1}becomesℓ2V​((ℓ1V)−1​u​ℓ1V)​(ℓ2V)−1.\ell_{2}^{V}\bigl((\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1}u\ell_{1}^{V}\bigr)(\ell_{2}^{V})^{-1}.

It is a Britton pinch in theℓ2V\ell_{2}^{V}-HNN extension exactly when(ℓ1V)−1​u​ℓ1V∈⟨w2⟩,(\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1}u\ell_{1}^{V}\in\langle w_{2}\rangle,

equivalently whenu∈W2−u\in W_{2}^{-}. Similarly, a subwordτ−1​u​τ\tau^{-1}u\taucontains(ℓ2V)−1​u​ℓ2V(\ell_{2}^{V})^{-1}u\ell_{2}^{V}

and pinches exactly whenu∈W2+u\in W_{2}^{+}. These are precisely the two
conditions excluded by reducedness of the surface HNN word. Adjacent
stable letters with the same sign do not form a Britton pinch. Hence
every word containing aτ\tau-letter maps to anℓ2V\ell_{2}^{V}-HNN-reduced, and therefore nontrivial, word. A word with
noτ\tau-letter lies inHHand is nontrivial becauseH↪L1↪LH\hookrightarrow L_{1}\hookrightarrow L. Britton’s lemma proves injectivity intoLL, and the two chronological applications ofSection5.1embedLLinG0G_{0}.
∎

In odd genus we writeS0=ρ0​(π1​(Σ2​r+1,p))=⟨U0,ℓ1V​U0​(ℓ1V)−1,ℓ2V​(ℓ1V)−1⟩.S_{0}=\rho_{0}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2r+1},p))=\left\langle U_{0},\ell_{1}^{V}U_{0}(\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1},\ell_{2}^{V}(\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1}\right\rangle.(39)

Both parity arguments prove that the push-off homomorphismρ0:π1​(Σg,p)⟶G0\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p)\longrightarrow G_{0}

is injective. We record next the knot-separation and homological
hypotheses used later.

## 5.4.Choice of knots and homological consequences

The preliminary knotsJ1D,…,JgDJ_{1}^{D},\ldots,J_{g}^{D}need only be oriented,
nontrivial, and hyperbolic. Every knot attached along a rank-two edge—the one or twoJνVJ_{\nu}^{V}and all later surgery knots—is chosen hyperbolic, and the
meridian-marked groups at these stages are pairwise nonisomorphic. No
pairwise-nonisomorphism condition is imposed on the
preliminaryKjDK_{j}^{D}. Choices also meeting the Alexander requirements
appear inSection15.4.

The following calculation supplies the homological normalization and
separates meridian powers from later push-off images.

## Proposition 5.6(First homology and push-off images).

For the preliminary groupA0A_{0}, for the exterior groupG0G_{0}after
the vertical-boundary surgeries, and for every exterior groupGiG_{i}obtained by the subsequent ordinary untwisted rim surgeries,H1​(A0;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩,H1​(Gi;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩(i≥0),H_{1}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle,\qquad H_{1}(G_{i};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle\qquad(i\geq 0),

andρ​(π1​(Σg,p))⊂[A0,A0],ρi​(π1​(Σg,p))⊂[Gi,Gi](i≥0).\rho(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p))\subset[A_{0},A_{0}],\qquad\rho_{i}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p))\subset[G_{i},G_{i}]\qquad(i\geq 0).

In particular, every later pre-surgery elementhi=ρi−1​(di)h_{i}=\rho_{i-1}(d_{i})is null-homologous.

## Proof.

EachKjDK_{j}^{D}abelianizes to the cyclic group generated by its meridian,
and every cyclic edge identifies that generator withμ\mu. Thus, as
also shown inSection4,H1​(A0;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩,H_{1}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle,

and every preliminary push-off is obtained from the initial trivial one by
inserting conjugates of null-homologous preferred longitudes, so its image
lies in[A0,A0][A_{0},A_{0}].

For a rank-two stage, writeG′=G∗⟨μ,h⟩(KJ×⟨s⟩),G^{\prime}=G*_{\langle\mu,h\rangle}\bigl(K_{J}\times\langle s\rangle\bigr),

withμ↦mJ\mu\mapsto m_{J},h↦sh\mapsto s, and assumeH1​(G;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩andρ​(π1​(Σg,p))⊂[G,G].H_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p))\subset[G,G].

Thenh=ρ​(d)h=\rho(d)is null-homologous. On the edge basis, the degree-one
difference mapH1​(⟨μ,h⟩)⟶H1​(G)⊕H1​(KJ×⟨s⟩),H_{1}(\langle\mu,h\rangle)\longrightarrow H_{1}(G)\oplus H_{1}(K_{J}\times\langle s\rangle),

isμ⟼([μ],−[mJ]),h⟼(0,−[s]).\mu\longmapsto([\mu],-[m_{J}]),\qquad h\longmapsto(0,-[s]).

Since[mJ],[s][m_{J}],[s]form a basis of the product-vertex summand, this map
is injective; so is the degree-zero difference mapℤ→ℤ⊕ℤ\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}. Mayer–Vietoris therefore identifiesH1​(G′;ℤ)H_{1}(G^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})with the cokernel, which identifies[mJ]=[μ][m_{J}]=[\mu]and kills[s][s]:H1​(G′;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩.H_{1}(G^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle.

BySection3.3, a new push-off is the preceding one
with conjugates ofℓJ\ell_{J}inserted in traversal order. In
abelianization the preceding portions recombine to the old zero class,
andℓJ\ell_{J}is null-homologous, so the new image lies in[G′,G′][G^{\prime},G^{\prime}].

At the first vertical stage the attaching element isw1∈U0⊂[A0,A0]w_{1}\in U_{0}\subset[A_{0},A_{0}]; in odd genus,w2∈U0w_{2}\in U_{0}persists
through the first amalgam. The argument therefore applies to all
vertical stages and then to every later surgery. Taking the loop to bedid_{i}gives[hi]=0[h_{i}]=0.
∎

ThusF0F_{0}has injective push-off and the homological hypotheses persist
through every later surgery. The next sections establish the local
Bass–Serre rigidity used to recognize the surgery curves.

## 6.Centralizers, transporters, and Bass–Serre axes

At a later ordinary untwisted rim-surgery stage, the one-step
edge subgroup isA=⟨μ,h⟩≅ℤ2,A=\langle\mu,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},

whereμ\muis the positive surface meridian andhhis the
pre-surgery push-off of the surgery curve. The fixed-subtree arguments
require bothCG​(x)=AC_{G}(x)=AandTransG⁡(x,A)=A\operatorname{Trans}_{G}(x,A)=Aforx∈A∖⟨μ⟩x\in A\setminus\langle\mu\rangle. This section records the axis
and reflection criteria, packages their consequences whenhhis not
a proper power, and isolates the minimal-subtree deduction used to
compare ambient and surface conjugacy.

All tree actions in this paper are simplicial and without inversions.
For a Bass–Serre tree, vertex and edge stabilizers are conjugate to the
corresponding vertex and edge groups. For an action of a groupGGon a treeTT, define the translation length ofx∈Gx\in Gby‖x‖T=minv∈V​(T)⁡dT​(v,x​v).\|x\|_{T}=\min_{v\in V(T)}d_{T}(v,xv).

The elementxxisellipticif it fixes a vertex, equivalently
if‖x‖T=0\|x\|_{T}=0, and ishyperbolicotherwise. A hyperbolic
element preserves a unique bi-infinite geodesic, itsaxisAxT⁡(x)\operatorname{Ax}_{T}(x), and acts on that axis by translation through‖x‖T\|x\|_{T}edges. For a subtreeL⊂TL\subset T, setFixGpt⁡(L)={g∈G:g​v=v​for every​v∈L}\operatorname{Fix}_{G}^{\mathrm{pt}}(L)=\{g\in G:gv=v\text{ for every }v\in L\}

andStabG⁡(L)={g∈G:g​L=L}.\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(L)=\{g\in G:gL=L\}.

IfLLis a line, an element ofStabG⁡(L)\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(L)is areflectionif its restriction toLLreverses the orientation
of the line.

## Definition 6.1.

For a subgroupA≤GA\leq Gand an elementx∈Gx\in G, thetransporter ofxxintoAAis the setTransG⁡(x,A)={g∈G:g−1​x​g∈A}.\operatorname{Trans}_{G}(x,A)=\{g\in G:g^{-1}xg\in A\}.

For a subgroupC≤GC\leq G, its commensurator isCommG⁡(C)={g∈G:C∩g​C​g−1​has finite index in both​C​and​g​C​g−1}.\operatorname{Comm}_{G}(C)=\{g\in G:C\cap gCg^{-1}\text{ has finite index in both }C\text{ and }gCg^{-1}\}.

IfC=⟨x⟩C=\langle x\rangleis infinite cyclic, theng∈CommG⁡(C)g\in\operatorname{Comm}_{G}(C)if and only if there existm,n∈ℤ∖{0}m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}such thatg​xm​g−1=xn.gx^{m}g^{-1}=x^{n}.

The integersmmandnnmay have either sign.

The following definition packages the two equalities used for every
later edge subgroup.

## Definition 6.2(Centralizer–transporter property).

LetGGbe a group with a distinguished elementμ\mu, and letA=⟨μ,h⟩≅ℤ2.A=\langle\mu,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

The subgroupAAhas thecentralizer–transporter property inGGrelative toμ\muif, for everyx∈A∖⟨μ⟩x\in A\setminus\langle\mu\rangle,CG​(x)=AandTransG⁡(x,A)=A.C_{G}(x)=A\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{Trans}_{G}(x,A)=A.

Equivalently, for everya,b∈ℤa,b\in\mathbb{Z}withb≠0b\neq 0,CG​(μa​hb)=⟨μ,h⟩C_{G}(\mu^{a}h^{b})=\langle\mu,h\rangle(40)

andTransG⁡(μa​hb,⟨μ,h⟩)=⟨μ,h⟩.\operatorname{Trans}_{G}(\mu^{a}h^{b},\langle\mu,h\rangle)=\langle\mu,h\rangle.(41)

Thus the property depends only on the inclusions⟨μ⟩≤A≤G\langle\mu\rangle\leq A\leq G, not on the choice of the second basis
element: replacinghhbyμk​h±1\mu^{k}h^{\pm 1}leaves it unchanged.
It is also preserved under simultaneous conjugation: for everyq∈Gq\in G, the triple(q​μ​q−1,q​h​q−1,q​A​q−1)(q\mu q^{-1},qhq^{-1},qAq^{-1})has the same property.

## Lemma 6.3(Torsion-freeness).

Let𝒢\mathcal{G}be a graph of groups with injective edge maps and
torsion-free vertex groups. Thenπ1​(𝒢)\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G})is
torsion-free. In particular, every group obtained in this manuscript
from knot groups, free groups, and infinite cyclic groups by the
amalgamated products, HNN extensions, and direct products withℤ\mathbb{Z}used in the construction is torsion-free.

## Proof.

A finite subgroup acting without inversions on a tree fixes a vertex[16, Example I.6.3.1]. Hence every finite-order element ofπ1​(𝒢)\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G})is conjugate into a vertex group and is therefore
trivial. Knot groups, free groups, and infinite cyclic groups are
torsion-free, as are their direct products withℤ\mathbb{Z}, so the
stated consequence follows inductively.
∎

The next criterion converts information about one axis into all three
group-theoretic conclusions needed later.

## Lemma 6.7(Cyclic syllable periodicity).

LetG=U∗EV,G=U*_{E}V,

and suppose that a hyperbolic elementxxhas a cyclically reduced
normal formx=u1​v1​⋯​uq​vq,ui∈U∖E,vi∈V∖E.x=u_{1}v_{1}\cdots u_{q}v_{q},\qquad u_{i}\in U\setminus E,\quad v_{i}\in V\setminus E.

Ifx=yn(n>1),x=y^{n}\qquad(n>1),

thenyyis hyperbolic, there is an integerr≥1r\geq 1such thatq=n​rq=nr, and, after a cyclic permutation, the cyclic sequence of
double cosetsE​u1​E,E​v1​E,…,E​uq​E,E​vq​EEu_{1}E,\ Ev_{1}E,\ldots,Eu_{q}E,\ Ev_{q}E

is annn-fold repetition of a sequence of length2​r2r.

In particular, ifxxhas syllable length four and is a proper power,
thenxxis the square of a hyperbolic element of syllable length two,
and its twoVV-syllables lie in the sameEE-double coset.

## Proof.

LetTTbe the Bass–Serre tree. Sincex=ynx=y^{n}is hyperbolic,yyis hyperbolic as well, andAxT(y)=AxT(x)=:L,∥x∥T=n∥y∥T.\operatorname{Ax}_{T}(y)=\operatorname{Ax}_{T}(x)=:L,\qquad\|x\|_{T}=n\|y\|_{T}.

The action preserves the two vertex types of the bipartite tree, so
every hyperbolic translation length is even. A cyclically reduced
normal form of syllable length2​q2qhas translation length2​q2q.
Writing‖y‖T=2​r\|y\|_{T}=2rtherefore givesq=n​rq=nr.

Choose a vertex ofLLand orientLLin the translation direction
ofyy. The segment from that vertex to itsxx-translate is the
concatenation of thenntranslates, by1,y,…,yn−11,y,\ldots,y^{n-1}, of a fundamental segment foryy. The
successive transitions throughUU- andVV-vertices record the
syllables of a cyclically reduced normal form. Changing the chosen
incident edge at a vertex multiplies the corresponding syllable on the
left or right by an element ofEE, so the intrinsic record is the
cyclic sequence ofEE-double cosets. The concatenation of the
fundamental segments therefore gives the assertednn-fold
periodicity. This is the tree formulation of the conjugacy theorem for
cyclically reduced amalgam words; compare[14, Chapter IV, §2].

Ifxxhas syllable length four, thenq=2=n​rq=2=nr. Sincen>1n>1andr≥1r\geq 1, one hasn=2n=2andr=1r=1. The twoVV-syllables therefore occupy the same position in the two repeated
periods and lie in the sameEE-double coset.
∎

## Lemma 7.6(The axis ofh1h_{1}in odd genus).

and, for everya,b∈ℤa,b\in\mathbb{Z}withb≠0b\neq 0,CG0​(μa​h1b)=A1,TransG0⁡(μa​h1b,A1)=A1.C_{G_{0}}(\mu^{a}h_{1}^{b})=A_{1},\qquad\operatorname{Trans}_{G_{0}}(\mu^{a}h_{1}^{b},A_{1})=A_{1}.

## Proof.

The graph-of-groups normal form forh1=ℓ2V​(ℓ1V)−1h_{1}=\ell_{2}^{V}(\ell_{1}^{V})^{-1}uses one non-edge syllable from each
leaf vertex group. Its cyclic normal-form path traverses each quotient

four. A fundamental segment of its axis contains vertices of both
leaf typesC1VC_{1}^{V}andC2VC_{2}^{V}.

Ifh1=vnh_{1}=v^{n}withn>1n>1, thenvvis hyperbolic on the same
axis and4=n​‖v‖T0.4=n\|v\|_{T_{0}}.

The action preserves the central and the two leaf vertex types, so
every positive translation length is even. Hencen=2n=2and‖v‖T0=2\|v\|_{T_{0}}=2. The axis of a length-two translation passes from
a central vertex through a leaf vertex and back to a central vertex.
Because the group action preserves each leaf-vertex orbit, every leaf
vertex on that axis has the same type. This contradicts the presence
of bothC1VC_{1}^{V}- andC2VC_{2}^{V}-type vertices onL1L_{1}. Thush1h_{1}is not a proper power.

At a centralA0A_{0}-type vertex of a fundamental segment, the two
consecutive axis-edge stabilizers areE1E_{1}andE2E_{2}, up to a
common conjugation. The direct-product description of the meridian
centralizer givesE1∩E2=⟨μ⟩×(⟨w1⟩∩⟨w2⟩)=⟨μ⟩.E_{1}\cap E_{2}=\langle\mu\rangle\times\bigl(\langle w_{1}\rangle\cap\langle w_{2}\rangle\bigr)=\langle\mu\rangle.

Indeed, using the basis in (34), the retraction
ofU0U_{0}that killsc=w2c=w_{2}sendsw1w_{1}to(∏j=1r[aj,bj])−1≠1\bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{r}[a_{j},b_{j}]\bigr)^{-1}\neq 1. Hence no nonzero
power ofw1w_{1}can equal a nonzero power ofw2w_{2}, and⟨w1⟩∩⟨w2⟩=1\langle w_{1}\rangle\cap\langle w_{2}\rangle=1.
At aCiVC_{i}^{V}-type vertex, the two consecutive edge stabilizers areEiandℓiV​Ei​(ℓiV)−1=Ei.E_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{i}^{V}E_{i}(\ell_{i}^{V})^{-1}=E_{i}.

The meridian commutes withℓ1V\ell_{1}^{V}andℓ2V\ell_{2}^{V}and belongs
to bothE1E_{1}andE2E_{2}, so it fixes every edge of a fundamental
segment and everyh1h_{1}-translate of that segment. Hence it fixesL1L_{1}pointwise. Conversely, an element fixingL1L_{1}pointwise
lies in the intersection at a central vertex and therefore belongs to⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangle.

The preceding paragraphs prove thath1h_{1}is hyperbolic, is not a
proper power, and has pointwise axis stabilizer⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangle.

For the selected pair, the cyclic surface-edge stabilizer⟨h⟩\langle h\ranglelies inA∩S′A\cap S^{\prime}. Conversely, an element ofA∩S′A\cap S^{\prime}fixes each of the two ambient edges, because no element of

The two-syllable normal form already gives the translation length. We

## 9.Centralizers in the amalgam arising from rim surgery

This section is group-theoretic. We classify the centralizer of every
nontrivial element in the one-edge amalgam introduced by an ordinary
untwisted rim surgery. The classification is organized by the fixed set
in the Bass–Serre tree: an axis, a single vertex, or a subtree containing
an edge. In the fixed-edge case, the exponents of the positive surface
meridian and the pre-surgery element determine the fixed subtree. The
resulting centralizers isolate the product vertex group and the meridian
centralizer, which are the two nonabelian fixed-edge centralizers relevant
to the recognition argument of the next section.

We begin with the hyperbolic knot-group facts needed on the product side.
LetJ⊂S3J\subset S^{3}be a hyperbolic knot, letK=π1​(E​(J)),K=\pi_{1}(E(J)),

and letPK=⟨m,ℓ⟩≅ℤ2P_{K}=\langle m,\ell\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

be its standard cusp subgroup, withmmthe knot meridian andℓ\ellthe
preferred longitude.

## Lemma 9.1(Centralizers and transporters in a hyperbolic knot group).

The groupKKis torsion-free and centerless. Every nontrivial element ofKKis either loxodromic or parabolic, and the following hold.
- (i)

Ifkkis loxodromic, thenCK​(k)=⟨r⟩≅ℤ,C_{K}(k)=\langle r\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z},

whererrgenerates the maximal cyclic subgroup containingkk.
- (ii)

Ifkkis parabolic, then it lies in a unique maximal parabolic
subgroupPP, andCK​(k)=P≅ℤ2.C_{K}(k)=P\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}.
- (iii)

The cusp subgroupPKP_{K}is malnormal and therefore
self-normalizing.
- (iv)

For everyn≠0n\neq 0,CK​(mn)=PKC_{K}(m^{n})=P_{K}

and{q∈K:q−1​mn​q∈⟨m⟩}=PK.\{q\in K:q^{-1}m^{n}q\in\langle m\rangle\}=P_{K}.

## Proof.

The complete hyperbolic structure realizesKKas a torsion-free,
finite-covolume discrete subgroup ofPSL2⁡(ℂ)\operatorname{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).
Thus every nontrivial element is loxodromic or parabolic. Ifkkis
loxodromic, every element commuting withkkpreserves its two fixed
points at infinity and hence its oriented invariant geodesic. The centralizer lies in the subgroup of orientation-preserving
isometries preserving this oriented geodesic, which is isomorphic toℝ×SO⁡(2)\mathbb{R}\times\operatorname{SO}(2). Projection to theℝ\mathbb{R}-factor is proper becauseSO⁡(2)\operatorname{SO}(2)is compact. SinceCK​(k)C_{K}(k)is a closed
discrete subgroup, its image is a closed subgroup ofℝ\mathbb{R}.
It is nonzero and countable, and is therefore an infinite cyclic
subgroup ofℝ\mathbb{R}. The kernel is a discrete subgroup of the
compact groupSO⁡(2)\operatorname{SO}(2), and hence is finite.
Torsion-freeness makes this kernel trivial. ThusCK​(k)C_{K}(k)is
infinite cyclic, generated by the element inducing the shortest
positive translation along the axis.

Ifkkis parabolic, every element commuting withkkfixes its unique
fixed point at infinity. It consequently lies in the maximal parabolic
subgroupPPstabilizing that point. For a finite-volume hyperbolic knot
complement,PPis free abelian of rank two, soP≤CK​(k)P\leq C_{K}(k)and henceCK​(k)=PC_{K}(k)=P. These centralizer descriptions also imply centerlessness:
a nontrivial central element would have centralizer all of the
non-elementary groupKK, whereas the two possibilities above are cyclic
or rank-two abelian. Torsion-freeness is already part of the manifold
holonomy realization.

For a hyperbolic knot, the standard cusp subgroup is malnormal by[6, Corollary 2]. IfqqnormalizesPKP_{K}, thenPK∩q​PK​q−1=PK≠1P_{K}\cap qP_{K}q^{-1}=P_{K}\neq 1, so malnormality givesq∈PKq\in P_{K}; thusPKP_{K}is self-normalizing. Sincemnm^{n}is a nontrivial parabolic,
its unique maximal parabolic subgroup isPKP_{K}, and thereforeCK​(mn)=PKC_{K}(m^{n})=P_{K}.

Finally, suppose thatq−1​mn​q∈⟨m⟩q^{-1}m^{n}q\in\langle m\rangle. This nontrivial
element belongs toq−1​PK​q∩PK.q^{-1}P_{K}q\cap P_{K}.

Malnormality givesq∈PKq\in P_{K}. Conversely, every element of the abelian
groupPKP_{K}centralizesmnm^{n}. This proves the transporter equality.
∎

The one-step decomposition ofTheorem3.1will be writtenH=G∗AB,A=⟨μ,h⟩≅ℤ2,B=K×⟨h⟩.H=G*_{A}B,\qquad A=\langle\mu,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},\qquad B=K\times\langle h\rangle.(58)

Hereμ\muis the positive surface meridian,hhis the pre-surgery
push-off image of the surgery curve, the knot meridianm∈Km\in Kis
identified withμ\mu, and the external central factor ofBBis
identified with⟨h⟩\langle h\rangle. PutD=⟨μ,ℓ,h⟩=PK×⟨h⟩≅ℤ3.D=\langle\mu,\ell,h\rangle=P_{K}\times\langle h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}.(59)

LetTTbe the Bass–Serre tree of (58). The
quotientH\TH\backslash Tis one edge with vertex groupsGGandBBand
edge groupAA. Thus theGG-vertices,BB-vertices, and edges ofTTare the cosetsH/GH/G,H/BH/B, andH/AH/A, and their stabilizers are the
corresponding conjugates ofGG,BB, andAA.

For the complete classification we assume:
- (C1)

AAhas the centralizer–transporter property inGGrelative toμ\muin the sense ofSection6;
- (C2)H1​(G;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩,[h]=0;H_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle,\qquad[h]=0;
- (C3)

withMG:=CG​(μ)M_{G}:=C_{G}(\mu), the groupMGM_{G}is nonabelian andCG​(μn)=MG(n≠0).C_{G}(\mu^{n})=M_{G}\qquad(n\neq 0).

Condition(C1)is the only one used for edge elements
with nonzerohh-exponent. Conditions(C2)and(C3)enter the transporter calculation for pure
meridian powers and the corresponding fixed-subtree case.

## Lemma 9.2(Transporter of a nonzero meridian power).

For everyn≠0n\neq 0,TransG⁡(μn,A)={g∈G:g−1​μn​g∈A}=MG.\operatorname{Trans}_{G}(\mu^{n},A)=\{g\in G:g^{-1}\mu^{n}g\in A\}=M_{G}.(60)

## Proof.

Letg−1​μn​g∈Ag^{-1}\mu^{n}g\in A. SinceA≅ℤ2A\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}, write the target uniquely
asg−1​μn​g=μa​hb.g^{-1}\mu^{n}g=\mu^{a}h^{b}.

Conjugation preserves abelianization. Condition(C2)therefore givesn​[μ]=a​[μ]+b​[h]=a​[μ],n[\mu]=a[\mu]+b[h]=a[\mu],

soa=na=n.

Suppose thatb≠0b\neq 0. By Condition(C1),CG​(μn​hb)=A,C_{G}(\mu^{n}h^{b})=A,

which is abelian. On the other hand,CG​(μn)=MGC_{G}(\mu^{n})=M_{G}is nonabelian by
Condition(C3). Centralizers of conjugate elements
are conjugate, so this is impossible. Henceb=0b=0, andg−1​μn​g=μn.g^{-1}\mu^{n}g=\mu^{n}.

It follows thatg∈CG​(μn)=MGg\in C_{G}(\mu^{n})=M_{G}.

Conversely, Condition(C3)givesMG=CG​(μn)M_{G}=C_{G}(\mu^{n}), and every element of this group conjugatesμn\mu^{n}to itself, which lies inAA.
∎

## Lemma 9.3(Centralizer from a fixed edge).

LetH=G∗ABH=G*_{A}B

## Proof.

SinceU∩A=A=V∩AU\cap A=A=V\cap A, the subgroup normal-form criterionSection5.1, with its smaller edge group equal toAA,
identifiesL=⟨U,V⟩L=\langle U,V\ranglewithU∗AVU*_{A}V. The groupsUUandVVcentralizexx, soL≤CH​(x)L\leq C_{H}(x)and everyLL-translate ofeeis fixed byxx.

and the transporter equality givesg∈Ug\in U. The analogous step at aBB-endpoint usesTransB⁡(x,A)=V\operatorname{Trans}_{B}(x,A)=V. Hence every fixed edge is anLL-translate ofee.

Ifc∈CH​(x)c\in C_{H}(x), thenc​eceis fixed, soc​e=l​ece=lefor somel∈Ll\in L.
Thusl−1​c∈StabH⁡(e)=A≤Ll^{-1}c\in\operatorname{Stab}_{H}(e)=A\leq L, provingCH​(x)=LC_{H}(x)=L.
Every fixed vertex lies on a fixed edge, becauseFixT⁡(x)\operatorname{Fix}_{T}(x)is
connected and containsee. The fixed edges form oneCH​(x)C_{H}(x)-orbit,

The fixed set of an elliptic element ofTTis a connected subtree. In
particular, an elliptic element that fixes no edge fixes exactly one
vertex. The next proposition applies this observation and the axis
conventions ofSection6to every nontrivial element ofHH.

## Proposition 9.4(Complete centralizer classification).

For every nontrivialx∈Hx\in H, exactly one of the following mutually
exclusive cases occurs, up to conjugacy.
- (i)

Hyperbolic onTT.There is an exact sequence1⟶Ex⟶CH​(x)⟶ℤ⟶1,1\longrightarrow E_{x}\longrightarrow C_{H}(x)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow 1,(61)

whereExE_{x}is free abelian of rank at most two. In particular,CH​(x)C_{H}(x)is metabelian.
- (ii)

Elliptic with one fixedGG-vertex.IfFixT⁡(x)={g​G},\operatorname{Fix}_{T}(x)=\{gG\},

thenCH​(x)=Cg​G​g−1​(x).C_{H}(x)=C_{gGg^{-1}}(x).
- (iii)

Elliptic with one fixedBB-vertex.After conjugating the fixed vertex to the baseBB-vertex, writex=(k,hn)∈K×⟨h⟩.x=(k,h^{n})\in K\times\langle h\rangle.

Thenk≠1k\neq 1andCH​(x)=CK​(k)×⟨h⟩.C_{H}(x)=C_{K}(k)\times\langle h\rangle.

Ifkkis loxodromic, this centralizer isℤ2\mathbb{Z}^{2}. Ifkkis
parabolic, conjugate further inBBso thatk∈PKk\in P_{K}; the one-vertex
hypothesis forcesk∉⟨μ⟩k\notin\langle\mu\rangle, and the centralizer isD≅ℤ3D\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}. In both alternatives the centralizer is abelian and is
therefore equal to its center.
- (iv)

Elliptic with a fixed edge.After conjugacy,x=μa​hb∈Ax=\mu^{a}h^{b}\in A, and exactly one of the following
three subcases occurs.
- (a)

Ifa=0a=0andb≠0b\neq 0, thenCH​(hb)=B=K×⟨h⟩.C_{H}(h^{b})=B=K\times\langle h\rangle.(62)
- (b)

Ifa​b≠0ab\neq 0, thenCH​(μa​hb)=D=⟨μ,ℓ,h⟩≅ℤ3.C_{H}(\mu^{a}h^{b})=D=\langle\mu,\ell,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}.(63)
- (c)

Ifa≠0a\neq 0andb=0b=0, thenCH​(μa)=MG∗AD.C_{H}(\mu^{a})=M_{G}*_{A}D.(64)

ConsequentlyCH​(μa)=CH​(μ)(a≠0).C_{H}(\mu^{a})=C_{H}(\mu)\qquad(a\neq 0).

## Proof.

We determine the centralizer from the action on the axis or fixed
subtree.

Hyperbolic elements.Suppose thatxxis hyperbolic and putL=AxT⁡(x)L=\operatorname{Ax}_{T}(x). Ifc∈CH​(x)c\in C_{H}(x),
thenc​L=AxT⁡(c​x​c−1)=AxT⁡(x)=L,cL=\operatorname{Ax}_{T}(cxc^{-1})=\operatorname{Ax}_{T}(x)=L,

so the centralizer preservesLL. The centralizer inIsom⁡(L)\operatorname{Isom}(L)of a
nonzero translation consists only of translations; in particular, no
element centralizingxxcan restrict to a reflection. The image ofCH​(x)⟶Isom⁡(L)C_{H}(x)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Isom}(L)

is therefore a subgroup of the infinite cyclic translation group of
the simplicial line. It contains the nonzero translation induced byxx, so it is a nonzero subgroup and hence isomorphic toℤ\mathbb{Z}.
Identifying this image with an abstractℤ\mathbb{Z}gives the right-hand map
in (61).

Its kernel isEx=CH​(x)∩FixHpt⁡(L).E_{x}=C_{H}(x)\cap\operatorname{Fix}_{H}^{\mathrm{pt}}(L).

An element fixingLLpointwise lies in the stabilizer of every edge ofLL. Each such edge stabilizer is a conjugate ofA≅ℤ2A\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}, soExE_{x}is a subgroup of a free abelian group of rank
two. HenceExE_{x}is free abelian of rank at most two. Since the
quotient is cyclic, the commutator subgroup ofCH​(x)C_{H}(x)lies in the

A unique fixed vertex.Suppose thatxxis elliptic and fixes no edge. Its fixed subtree is

To finish, every nontrivial element is hyperbolic or elliptic. An
elliptic element that fixes no edge fixes exactly one vertex; if it
fixed two vertices, it would fix their intervening geodesic and hence
an edge. The action preserves the two vertex types, so the singletonGG- andBB-vertex cases are disjoint. Every edge-fixing element is
conjugate intoAA, and the
three displayed exponent conditions exhaust all nonzero pairs(a,b)(a,b).
Their fixed-subtree descriptions are mutually exclusive even if the
element fixes more than one edge. Thehh-power and mixed cases both
have a uniqueBB-vertex, but their centralizers are respectively the
nonabelian groupBBand the abelian groupDD. In the meridian case,AAis proper in the nonabelian groupMGM_{G}, so the fixed edges at aGG-vertex include at least two distinctMG/AM_{G}/Acosets and the fixed
subtree has more than oneBB-vertex. Consequently no element can
belong, up to conjugacy, to two different listed cases.
∎

The center is immediate in every abelian case of the classification.
The two nonabelian centralizers arising in the fixed-edge cases require
separate arguments: the product vertex groupBBhas center⟨h⟩\langle h\rangle,
whereas the meridian centralizer has center⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangleunder
the additional hypotheses below. We first exclude the hyperbolic case
from having a hyperbolic knot-group central quotient.

## Corollary 9.5(Central quotients of hyperbolic centralizers).

Ifxxis hyperbolic onTT, thenCH​(x)/Z​(CH​(x))C_{H}(x)/Z(C_{H}(x))

is solvable. In particular, it is not isomorphic to a nonabelian
hyperbolic knot group.

## Proof.

BySection9, the groupCH​(x)C_{H}(x)is
metabelian. Every quotient of a metabelian group is metabelian and
therefore solvable. A finite-volume hyperbolic knot group is
non-elementary and contains a nonabelian free subgroup, obtained by
ping-pong from two loxodromic elements with disjoint fixed-point sets.
Since every subgroup of a solvable group is solvable and a nonabelian
free group is not solvable, a nonabelian hyperbolic knot group cannot be
solvable.
∎

## Lemma 9.7(Center of a nondegenerate amalgam).

The fixed-set classification now gives the intrinsic separation needed
in the next section. Among centralizers not inherited from theGG-vertex, hyperbolic elements have solvable central quotients, the
unique-BB-vertex and mixed edge cases have abelian centralizers, and
the meridian centralizer has central quotient of first Betti number
greater than one under the preceding hypotheses. The only remaining
centralizer whose quotient by its center can be a nonabelian hyperbolic knot group isCH​(hb)=B,Z​(B)=⟨h⟩,B/Z​(B)≅K,C_{H}(h^{b})=B,\qquad Z(B)=\langle h\rangle,\qquad B/Z(B)\cong K,

which is precisely the product vertex group recognized inSection10.

## 10.Product vertex groups determined by meridian-marked
centralizer pairs

This section is group-theoretic. The preceding section classifies
centralizers in one rim-surgery amalgam. We now use that classification
inductively to recognize every product vertex group from the ambient
group together with the distinguished meridian. The recognition datum
is the simultaneous conjugacy class of a centralizer and a meridian
inside it, together with the induced quotient by the center. We also
show that this characterization persists under later amalgamations and
is therefore preserved by every automorphism fixing the meridian.

Recall that a meridian-marked group is a pair(Q,μ)(Q,\mu)with a
distinguished element. An isomorphism of meridian-marked groups sends
the distinguished element literally to the distinguished element in
the target. Vertex groups in a graph-of-groups decomposition, on the
other hand, are determined only up to conjugacy in the ambient group.

## Definition 10.1(The ordered rank-two surgery groups).

Fix a finite initial segment of the construction, and enumerate its
rim surgeries with rank-two edge group in chronological order: first
the one vertical-boundary surgery in even genus or the two
vertical-boundary surgeries in odd genus, and then the specified
ordered rim surgeries alongd1,d2,…,dNd_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{N}. WriteΓ0=A0\Gamma_{0}=A_{0}

and, for1≤r≤R1\leq r\leq R, at therr-th rank-two attachment writeΓr=Γr−1∗ErBr,Er=⟨μ,hr⟩,Br=Kr×⟨zr⟩,\Gamma_{r}=\Gamma_{r-1}*_{E_{r}}B_{r},\qquad E_{r}=\langle\mu,h_{r}\rangle,\qquad B_{r}=K_{r}\times\langle z_{r}\rangle,

wherezrz_{r}is identified withhrh_{r}and the chosen meridianmr∈Krm_{r}\in K_{r}is identified withμ\mu. Thus, in even genus,Γ1=G0\Gamma_{1}=G_{0}, while in odd genusΓ1=G0(1),Γ2=G0.\Gamma_{1}=G_{0}^{(1)},\qquad\Gamma_{2}=G_{0}.

In even genus,Γi+1=Gi(0≤i≤N),\Gamma_{i+1}=G_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq N),

whereas in odd genus,Γi+2=Gi(0≤i≤N).\Gamma_{i+2}=G_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq N).

All edge maps are injective, so the normal-form theorem gives canonical
inclusionsΓr−1↪Γr.\Gamma_{r-1}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{r}.

We therefore regard every earlier groupΓs\Gamma_{s}and every earlier
product vertex groupBsB_{s}as subgroups ofΓr\Gamma_{r}whenevers≤rs\leq r. This notation
only reindexes the groups occurring in the construction; it does not
define a larger abstract class of groups. The preliminary knot-group
factorsKjDK_{j}^{D}inA0A_{0}are not among theKrK_{r}.

We begin with the meridian centralizer in the cyclic-edge preliminary
group.

Letc=ℓjDc=\ell_{j}^{D}be the preferred longitude in the factorKjDK_{j}^{D}ofA0=K1D∗⟨μ⟩⋯∗⟨μ⟩KgD.A_{0}=K_{1}^{D}*_{\langle\mu\rangle}\cdots*_{\langle\mu\rangle}K_{g}^{D}.

## Lemma 10.4(Centralizer–transporter property for the
vertical-boundary edge groups).

In even genus,⟨μ,w⟩\langle\mu,w\ranglehas the
centralizer–transporter property inA0A_{0}. In odd genus,⟨μ,w1⟩\langle\mu,w_{1}\ranglehas this property inA0A_{0}, and⟨μ,w2⟩\langle\mu,w_{2}\ranglehas this property inG0(1)=A0∗E1C1V,E1=⟨μ,w1⟩,G_{0}^{(1)}=A_{0}*_{E_{1}}C_{1}^{V},\qquad E_{1}=\langle\mu,w_{1}\rangle,

before the second vertical-boundary surgery is performed.

## Proof.

We first prove a uniform statement inA0A_{0}. Letv∈U0∖{1}v\in U_{0}\setminus\{1\}be an element that is not a proper power.
Then⟨μ,v⟩\langle\mu,v\ranglehas the centralizer–transporter property
inA0A_{0}.

LetTDT_{D}be the Bass–Serre tree of the cyclic-edge decomposition ofA0A_{0}. The action ofCA0​(μ)/⟨μ⟩≅U0C_{A_{0}}(\mu)/\langle\mu\rangle\cong U_{0}

onFixTD⁡(μ)\operatorname{Fix}_{T_{D}}(\mu)is the Bass–Serre action forU0=⟨ℓ1D⟩∗⋯∗⟨ℓgD⟩.U_{0}=\langle\ell_{1}^{D}\rangle*\cdots*\langle\ell_{g}^{D}\rangle.

There are two cases.

Suppose first thatvvis hyperbolic in this free-product tree, and letLLbe its axis. Every edge ofFixTD⁡(μ)\operatorname{Fix}_{T_{D}}(\mu)has stabilizer
exactly⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangleinA0A_{0}. Indeed, its stabilizer is a

forces the conjugate generator to beμ\mu, rather than a distinct
power. HenceFixA0pt⁡(L)=⟨μ⟩.\operatorname{Fix}_{A_{0}}^{\mathrm{pt}}(L)=\langle\mu\rangle.

If an element ofA0A_{0}preservesLLsetwise, it normalizes this
pointwise stabilizer. It therefore conjugatesμ\mutoμ±1\mu^{\pm 1}, andH1​(A0;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩H_{1}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangleforces the positive
sign. Thus the setwise stabilizer lies inCA0​(μ)C_{A_{0}}(\mu).

After quotienting by⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangle, the pointwise stabilizer ofLLis trivial. Hence the orientation-preserving setwise stabilizer
ofLLembeds in the translation group of the simplicial line and is

The next results control conjugacy across one amalgam. Their purpose is
to retain the exponent pair of an edge element and, in particular, to
reflect ambient meridian conjugacy back into a vertex group.

## Lemma 10.6(Conjugacy rigidity inside the edge group).

LetH=G∗AB,A=⟨μ,h⟩≅ℤ2,B=K×⟨h⟩,H=G*_{A}B,\qquad A=\langle\mu,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},\qquad B=K\times\langle h\rangle,

whereAAhas the centralizer–transporter property inGGrelative toμ\mu, whereTransG⁡(μn,A)=CG​(μn)(n≠0),\operatorname{Trans}_{G}(\mu^{n},A)=C_{G}(\mu^{n})\qquad(n\neq 0),

and whereKKis a hyperbolic knot group with standard cusp subgroupPK=⟨μ,ℓ⟩P_{K}=\langle\mu,\ell\rangle. Ifa,a′∈Aa,a^{\prime}\in Aare conjugate inHH,
thena=a′a=a^{\prime}.

## Proof.

ApplySection10. It is enough to prove that a
single vertex-group conjugacy taking an element ofAAback intoAAfixes that element. Write the edge element asμp​hq.\mu^{p}h^{q}.

Suppose first that the conjugator lies inGG. Ifq≠0q\neq 0, the
centralizer–transporter property places the conjugator inAA, whose

Now let the conjugator lie inBB, and write it as(k,hr)(k,h^{r}). Since
the externalhh-factor is central,(k,hr)−1​μp​hq​(k,hr)=k−1​μp​k​hq.(k,h^{r})^{-1}\mu^{p}h^{q}(k,h^{r})=k^{-1}\mu^{p}k\,h^{q}.

central inBB. Ifp≠0p\neq 0and the displayed conjugate belongs toAA, thenk−1​μp​k∈⟨μ⟩.k^{-1}\mu^{p}k\in\langle\mu\rangle.

The knot-group transporter equality inSection9givesk∈PKk\in P_{K}. SincePKP_{K}is
abelian,k−1​μp​k=μpk^{-1}\mu^{p}k=\mu^{p}. Hence the exponent pair(p,q)(p,q)is
unchanged in every vertex-group step, and the conjugacy chain consists

Under the hypotheses ofSection10,(H⋅μ)∩G=G⋅μ(H\mathbin{\cdot}\mu)\cap G=G\mathbin{\cdot}\mu

immediate.

We now define the recognition datum. The use of one common conjugator
is essential: it retains the distinguished meridian inside the same
centralizer rather than independently conjugating the two entries.

## Definition 10.8(Meridian-marked centralizer pairs).

Let(Q,μ)(Q,\mu)be a meridian-marked group, and let(K,m)(K,m)be a
meridian-marked nonabelian hyperbolic knot group. Two pairs(C,u)(C,u)and(C′,u′)(C^{\prime},u^{\prime}), withC,C′≤QC,C^{\prime}\leq Qandu∈Cu\in C,u′∈C′u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}, aresimultaneously conjugateif there is oneq∈Qq\in Qsuch thatC′=q​C​q−1,u′=q​u​q−1.C^{\prime}=qCq^{-1},\qquad u^{\prime}=quq^{-1}.

A simultaneous conjugacy class of pairs(C,u)(C,u)inQQis said torealize(K,m)(K,m)if:
- (i)

C=CQ​(x)C=C_{Q}(x)for some nontrivialx∈Qx\in Q;
- (ii)

u∈Cu\in Cis conjugate inQQtoμ\mu;
- (iii)

CCis nonabelian;
- (iv)

Z​(C)Z(C)is infinite cyclic;
- (v)

the quotient pair satisfies(C/Z​(C),u¯)≅(K,m)(C/Z(C),\bar{u})\cong(K,m)

as a meridian-marked group.

No generator ofZ​(C)Z(C)is chosen. In condition(v), the
isomorphism sends the distinguished elementu¯\bar{u}literally tomm.

Every condition inSection10is preserved by an
automorphismΦ∈Aut⁡(Q)\Phi\in\operatorname{Aut}(Q)withΦ​(μ)=μ\Phi(\mu)=\mu. Indeed,Φ​(CQ​(x))=CQ​(Φ​(x)),Φ​(Z​(C))=Z​(Φ​(C)),\Phi(C_{Q}(x))=C_{Q}(\Phi(x)),\qquad\Phi(Z(C))=Z(\Phi(C)),

andΦ\Phiinduces an isomorphismC/Z​(C)⟶Φ​(C)/Z​(Φ​(C)).C/Z(C)\longrightarrow\Phi(C)/Z(\Phi(C)).

Ifuuis conjugate toμ\mu, thenΦ​(u)\Phi(u)is also conjugate toμ\mu. Composing the quotient isomorphism induced byΦ−1\Phi^{-1}with the marked isomorphism in condition (v) shows that(Φ​(C),Φ​(u))(\Phi(C),\Phi(u))realizes the same meridian-marked knot group.

## Theorem 10.9(Recognition of the new product vertex group).

LetH=G∗⟨μ,h⟩(K×⟨h⟩)H=G*_{\langle\mu,h\rangle}(K\times\langle h\rangle)

be the amalgam (58), whereKKis a nonabelian
hyperbolic knot group with chosen meridianm=μm=\mu. Assume
Conditions(C1),(C2), and(C3). In addition, forMG=CG​(μ),M_{G}=C_{G}(\mu),

assumeZ​(MG)=⟨μ⟩,b1​(MG/⟨μ⟩)≥g≥3.Z(M_{G})=\langle\mu\rangle,\qquad b_{1}(M_{G}/\langle\mu\rangle)\geq g\geq 3.

Suppose that the meridian-marked nonabelian hyperbolic knot groups
realized by pairs inGGform a specified collection{(Ki,mi):i∈I},\{(K_{i},m_{i}):i\in I\},

and that, for eachii, there is exactly one simultaneous conjugacy
class of pairs inGGrealizing(Ki,mi)(K_{i},m_{i}). Assume these marked knot
groups are pairwise nonisomorphic and that(K,m)(K,m)is not isomorphic
to any of them.

PutB=K×⟨h⟩.B=K\times\langle h\rangle.

Then every pair inHHrealizing a meridian-marked nonabelian
hyperbolic knot group is simultaneously conjugate either to a pair
lying inGGand realizing one of the(Ki,mi)(K_{i},m_{i}), or to(B,μ)(B,\mu).
In particular, the pairs inHHrealizing(K,m)(K,m)are exactly the
simultaneous conjugates of(B,μ)(B,\mu).

## Proof.

Let(C,u)(C,u)realize a meridian-marked nonabelian hyperbolic knot group,
withC=CH​(x).C=C_{H}(x).

Apply the complete centralizer classification ofSection9, conjugating the pair(C,u)(C,u)by the
same element whenever the classification conjugatesxx.

Ifxxis hyperbolic on the Bass–Serre tree, thenC/Z​(C)C/Z(C)is solvable bySection9. It cannot be
a nonabelian hyperbolic knot group.

Ifxxfixes a uniqueBB-vertex and is not a nonzero power ofhh,
thenCCisℤ2\mathbb{Z}^{2}orℤ3\mathbb{Z}^{3}and is abelian. Ifx=μa​hbx=\mu^{a}h^{b}witha​b≠0ab\neq 0, thenC=D≅ℤ3.C=D\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}.

These cases fail the nonabelian condition inSection10.

Ifxxis a nonzero meridian power, thenC=CH​(μ)=MG∗⟨μ,h⟩D.C=C_{H}(\mu)=M_{G}*_{\langle\mu,h\rangle}D.

BySection9,Z​(C)=⟨μ⟩Z(C)=\langle\mu\rangle

andb1​(C/Z​(C))=b1​(MG/⟨μ⟩)+1≥g+1.b_{1}(C/Z(C))=b_{1}(M_{G}/\langle\mu\rangle)+1\geq g+1.

A classical knot group has first Betti number one, so this central
quotient is not a knot group.

Suppose thatxxfixes a uniqueGG-vertex. Simultaneously conjugate(C,u)(C,u)so that this vertex is the baseGG-vertex. ThenC=CG​(x)≤G,u∈G.C=C_{G}(x)\leq G,\qquad u\in G.

The elementuuremainsHH-conjugate toμ\mu.Section10upgrades this to conjugacy insideGG.
Thus(C,u)(C,u)is a pair in the meridian-marked group(G,μ)(G,\mu), and the
hypothesis on realizations inGGmakes it simultaneously conjugate to
the unique pair realizing one of the(Ki,mi)(K_{i},m_{i}).

The only remaining case isx=hb,b≠0.x=h^{b},\qquad b\neq 0.

The classification givesCH​(hb)=B,C_{H}(h^{b})=B,

andSection9givesZ​(B)=⟨h⟩.Z(B)=\langle h\rangle.

Ifu∈Bu\in BisHH-conjugate toμ\mu, thenSection10makes itBB-conjugate toμ\mu.
Chooseq∈Bq\in Bwithq−1​u​q=μq^{-1}uq=\mu. Since conjugation byqqpreservesBBsetwise, the pair(B,u)(B,u)is simultaneously conjugate
to(B,μ)(B,\mu). Finally,(B/Z​(B),μ¯)≅(K,m),(B/Z(B),\bar{\mu})\cong(K,m),

so(B,μ)(B,\mu)realizes(K,m)(K,m).

The cases above are exhaustive. Pairwise nonisomorphism is used only
at the final identification: none of the pairs inherited fromGGrealizes the new marked knot group(K,m)(K,m). Hence the simultaneous
conjugates of(B,μ)(B,\mu)are exactly the pairs inHHrealizing(K,m)(K,m).
∎

The preliminary cyclic amalgam supplies the induction base. Its
knot-group factors are vertex groups, but they do not occur as the
nonabelian centralizers required by the recognition datum.

## Proposition 10.10(Centralizer pairs in the preliminary group).

No pair inA0A_{0}realizes a meridian-marked nonabelian hyperbolic knot
group.

## Proof.

LetTDT_{D}be the Bass–Serre tree of the cyclic-edge graph of groups forA0=K1D∗⟨μ⟩⋯∗⟨μ⟩KgD,A_{0}=K_{1}^{D}*_{\langle\mu\rangle}\cdots*_{\langle\mu\rangle}K_{g}^{D},

and letx≠1x\neq 1.

Ifxxis hyperbolic onTDT_{D}, its centralizer preserves its axis. The
pointwise kernel is contained in a cyclic edge stabilizer, and the
translation image is cyclic. Thus the centralizer is metabelian, and
its quotient by its center is solvable. A nonabelian hyperbolic knot
group is not solvable.

Suppose thatxxis elliptic and is not conjugate to a nonzero power ofμ\mu. It fixes no edge, because all edge stabilizers are conjugates
of⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangle. Its fixed subtree is therefore a single
vertex stabilized by one of the knot-group factorsKjDK_{j}^{D}. The full
centralizer lies in that vertex group. BySection9, it is cyclic for a loxodromic element and
rank-two abelian for a parabolic element. In either case it fails the
nonabelian-centralizer condition.

Finally, ifxxis conjugate toμn\mu^{n}withn≠0n\neq 0, thenSection10givesCA0​(x)≅⟨μ⟩×U0.C_{A_{0}}(x)\cong\langle\mu\rangle\times U_{0}.

Its center is the meridian factor, and its central quotient isU0U_{0},
whose first Betti number isg≥3g\geq 3. A knot group has first Betti
number one. No case realizes a meridian-marked nonabelian hyperbolic
knot group.
∎

We next prove that a product vertex group recognized at an earlier
attachment remains a centralizer after a later attachment. The
hypotheses concerning the meridian centralizer are stated explicitly so
that this result does not rely on a second, implicit induction.

## Lemma 10.11(Persistence of earlier product-vertex centralizers).

Let(G,μ)(G,\mu)be a meridian-marked group, letA=⟨μ,h⟩≅ℤ2A=\langle\mu,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

have the centralizer–transporter property inGG, and assume thatCG​(μa)=MG:=CG​(μ)(a≠0),C_{G}(\mu^{a})=M_{G}:=C_{G}(\mu)\qquad(a\neq 0),Z​(MG)=⟨μ⟩,b1​(MG/⟨μ⟩)≥g≥3.Z(M_{G})=\langle\mu\rangle,\qquad b_{1}(M_{G}/\langle\mu\rangle)\geq g\geq 3.

Suppose an earlier product vertex groupBj=Kj×⟨zj⟩B_{j}=K_{j}\times\langle z_{j}\rangle

satisfies, for everyn≠0n\neq 0,CG​(zjn)=Bj,Z​(Bj)=⟨zj⟩,Bj/Z​(Bj)≅Kj,C_{G}(z_{j}^{n})=B_{j},\qquad Z(B_{j})=\langle z_{j}\rangle,\qquad B_{j}/Z(B_{j})\cong K_{j},

whereKjK_{j}is a nonabelian hyperbolic knot group. Form the later
amalgamH=G∗A(K×⟨h⟩).H=G*_{A}(K\times\langle h\rangle).

Then no nonzero power ofzjz_{j}is conjugate inGGintoAA, andCH​(zjn)=CG​(zjn)=Bj(n≠0).C_{H}(z_{j}^{n})=C_{G}(z_{j}^{n})=B_{j}\qquad(n\neq 0).

## Proof.

Suppose that, for someq∈Gq\in Gandn≠0n\neq 0,q−1​zjn​q=μa​hb.q^{-1}z_{j}^{n}q=\mu^{a}h^{b}.

The target is nontrivial. Ifb≠0b\neq 0, the
centralizer–transporter property givesCG​(μa​hb)=A,C_{G}(\mu^{a}h^{b})=A,

which is abelian, whereasCG​(zjn)=BjC_{G}(z_{j}^{n})=B_{j}is nonabelian. This is
impossible because conjugate elements have conjugate centralizers.

Ifb=0b=0, thena≠0a\neq 0andCG​(μa)=MG.C_{G}(\mu^{a})=M_{G}.

Conjugation would give an isomorphismBj≅MG.B_{j}\cong M_{G}.

Any group isomorphism carries the center onto the center and therefore
induces an isomorphism of central quotients. Hereb1​(Bj/Z​(Bj))=b1​(Kj)=1,b_{1}(B_{j}/Z(B_{j}))=b_{1}(K_{j})=1,

whereasb1​(MG/Z​(MG))=b1​(MG/⟨μ⟩)≥g.b_{1}(M_{G}/Z(M_{G}))=b_{1}(M_{G}/\langle\mu\rangle)\geq g.

This is again impossible. Thus no nonzero power ofzjz_{j}has aGG-conjugate inAA.

LetTTbe the Bass–Serre tree of the new amalgam. The elementzjnz_{j}^{n}fixes the baseGG-vertex. If it fixed any edge, connectedness
of its fixed subtree would give a fixed edge incident to that vertex.
Such an edge is represented byq​AqAwithq∈Gq\in G, and its fixedness
is equivalent toq−1​zjn​q∈Aq^{-1}z_{j}^{n}q\in A, which was just excluded. HenceFixT⁡(zjn)={G}.\operatorname{Fix}_{T}(z_{j}^{n})=\{G\}.

Every centralizing element preserves this unique vertex, and thereforeCH​(zjn)=CG​(zjn)=Bj.C_{H}(z_{j}^{n})=C_{G}(z_{j}^{n})=B_{j}.

∎

The preceding results can now be iterated without circularity. The
induction simultaneously maintains the earlier product centralizers,
the exact collection of realized marked knot groups, and the full
meridian-centralizer information required at the next attachment.

## Theorem 10.12(Recognition of all product vertex groups).

Use the ordered groupsΓr\Gamma_{r}ofSection10, and suppose that the meridian-marked hyperbolic
knot groups(Kr,mr)(K_{r},m_{r})used in all vertical-boundary and subsequent

the following statements hold inΓr\Gamma_{r}.

- (ii)

The simultaneous conjugacy class of(Bs,μ)(B_{s},\mu)is the unique
class inΓr\Gamma_{r}realizing(Ks,ms)(K_{s},m_{s}).
- (iii)

Every automorphismΦ∈Aut⁡(Γr)\Phi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma_{r})satisfyingΦ​(μ)=μ\Phi(\mu)=\mupreserves the conjugacy class of eachBsB_{s}.
- (iv)

For everyn≠0n\neq 0,CΓr​(μn)=CΓr​(μ).C_{\Gamma_{r}}(\mu^{n})=C_{\Gamma_{r}}(\mu).
- (v)

## Remark 10.13(Role of pairwise nonisomorphism).

The complete centralizer classification leaves only the following
possibilities for a centralizer pair with a nonabelian centralizer:
central quotients that are solvable, meridian-centralizer quotients with
first Betti number at leastgg, pairs inherited from earlier product
vertex groups, and the pair associated to the newly introduced product
vertex group. The remaining centralizer cases are abelian. Pairwise
nonisomorphism of the meridian-marked hyperbolic knot groups therefore
distinguishes the product vertex groups introduced at different
rank-two surgery stages. It is not used to prove the centralizer
formulas themselves.

Thus the final meridian-marked group determines, for every
vertical-boundary or subsequent rank-two rim surgery, the unique
simultaneous conjugacy class of the corresponding product vertex group
and a meridian inside it. In particular, it determines the conjugacy
class of that product vertex group and its infinite cyclic center. The
next section passes from this conjugacy-invariant subgroup to an
automorphism-invariant normal subgroup.

## 11.Commutator quotients invariant under automorphisms fixing the meridian

Recognition determines a product vertex group only up to conjugacy,
whereas reverse induction requires a literal automorphism-invariant
quotient. The normal closure of the product vertex group’s commutator
subgroup supplies that quotient.

Fix1≤i≤N1\leq i\leq N. Hereiiindexes the ordered rim surgeries
along the curvesd1,…,dNd_{1},\ldots,d_{N}, not the preliminary surgeries
along meridian-disk boundaries or the vertical boundary. At theii-th ordered stage,Gi=Gi−1∗AiBi,Ai=⟨μ,hi⟩,Bi=Ki×⟨zi⟩,zi=hi.G_{i}=G_{i-1}*_{A_{i}}B_{i},\qquad A_{i}=\langle\mu,h_{i}\rangle,\qquad B_{i}=K_{i}\times\langle z_{i}\rangle,\qquad z_{i}=h_{i}.

ByTheorem10.12, the simultaneous conjugacy class of(Bi,μ)(B_{i},\mu), and hence the conjugacy class ofBiB_{i}, is uniquely
determined by the meridian-marked group(Gi,μ)(G_{i},\mu).

## Definition 11.1(Invariance under meridian-fixing automorphisms).

An automorphismΦ∈Aut⁡(G)\Phi\in\operatorname{Aut}(G)of a meridian-marked group(G,μ)(G,\mu)ismeridian-fixingifΦ​(μ)=μ\Phi(\mu)=\mu. A normal
subgroupN​⊴​GN\trianglelefteq Gisinvariant under
meridian-fixing automorphismsifΦ​(N)=N\Phi(N)=N

for every meridian-fixingΦ\Phi. A conjugacy class of subgroups is
invariant under meridian-fixing automorphisms if every suchΦ\Phisends each representative to a conjugate representative. These are
different assertions: the first is literal subgroup invariance, while
the second is invariance only of a conjugacy class.
