Thus a meridian-fixing automorphism descends while retaining the same
surface-group action. We next choose an ordered curve family that
detects every mapping class.

## 12.Labelled filling systems

Reverse induction also requires an ordered curve family whose
consecutive members meet once and whose labelled unoriented stabilizer
is trivial. We construct a filling chain with finite stabilizer and
append curves until that stabilizer vanishes.

Throughout this section, curves are considered as unoriented isotopy
classes, andi​(c,d)i(c,d)denotes geometric intersection number.

## Definition 12.1(Ordered labelled filling systems).

A finite family of essential simple closed curvesfillsΣg\Sigma_{g}if, after choosing representatives simultaneously in
pairwise minimal position, every component of the complement of their
union is a disk. Anordered labelled unoriented filling systemis a tuple𝒟=(d1,…,dN)\mathcal{D}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{N})

of pairwise distinct unoriented isotopy classes of essential simple
closed curves that fillsΣg\Sigma_{g}. The indexjjis the label ofdjd_{j}; no permutation of labels is allowed. Its labelled unoriented
stabilizer isStabMod⁡(Σg)⁡(𝒟labelled,unoriented)={φ∈Mod⁡(Σg):φ​(dj)=dj​for every​j}.\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{labelled,unoriented}})=\{\varphi\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}):\varphi(d_{j})=d_{j}\text{ for every }j\}.

The same order1,…,N1,\ldots,Nprescribes the order of the rim
surgeries.

## Lemma 12.2(Filling chain).

Letd1d_{1}be any nonseparating simple closed curve onΣg\Sigma_{g}.
There exist nonseparating curvesd1,d2,…,d2​gd_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{2g}

such thati​(dj,dj+1)=1,i​(dj,dk)=0if​|j−k|>1,i(d_{j},d_{j+1})=1,\qquad i(d_{j},d_{k})=0\quad\text{if }|j-k|>1,

and their union fillsΣg\Sigma_{g}.

## Proof.

Plumb annuliA1,…,AnA_{1},\ldots,A_{n}successively along squares so thatAjA_{j}meetsAkA_{k}exactly when|j−k|=1|j-k|=1, and letaja_{j}be
their cores. The regular neighborhoodNnN_{n}is obtained from one
annulus byn−1n-1square plumbings, soχ​(Nn)=1−n.\chi(N_{n})=1-n.

The first neighborhood has two boundary components. At the terminal
annulus, the next plumbing band joins distinct boundary components whennnis odd and two arcs of the same component whennnis even;
hence#​∂Nn={2,n​odd,1,n​even.\#\partial N_{n}=\begin{cases}2,&n\text{ odd},\\
1,&n\text{ even}.\end{cases}

ThusN2​gN_{2g}has one boundary component and Euler characteristic1−2​g1-2g, so it has genusgg. Capping its boundary by a disk gives
a closed genus-ggsurface, which we identify withΣg\Sigma_{g}.
Under this identification,N2​gN_{2g}is a regular neighborhood of the
chain and its complement is the capping disk, so the cores fillΣg\Sigma_{g}. Eachaja_{j}is nonseparating because it meets an
adjacent core once, whereas a separating curve has zero mod-two
intersection with every closed curve.

Finally, the change-of-coordinates principle gives an
orientation-preserving mapping class carryinga1a_{1}to the prescribedd1d_{1}; applying it to the whole chain proves the lemma.
∎

## Lemma 12.3(Finite stabilizer of a filling system).

The labelled unoriented stabilizer of a finite filling system is
finite.

## Proof.

Choose representativesγ1,…,γm\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}simultaneously in
pairwise minimal position. Make all intersections transverse and
remove triple intersection points by small local perturbations. Their
unionΓ=γ1∪⋯∪γm\Gamma=\gamma_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\gamma_{m}

is a finite labelled ribbon graph: every edge records the label of the
curve containing it, and the orientation ofΣg\Sigma_{g}supplies the
cyclic order of the incident half-edges at every vertex. Since the
curves fill, the complementary regions are disks, soΓ\Gammais the
one-skeleton of a finite cell decomposition ofΣg\Sigma_{g}.

We justify the simultaneous representative used below. Letφ\varphifix every labelled unoriented curve class and letffrepresentφ\varphi. The systemf​(Γ)f(\Gamma)is again pairwise
minimal and has the same labelled component classes asΓ\Gamma.
Straighten its components inductively. Afterf​(γ1),…,f​(γj−1)f(\gamma_{1}),\ldots,f(\gamma_{j-1})have been carried toγ1,…,γj−1\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{j-1}setwise, cut the surface along the
already straightened union, replacing its crossing vertices by small
vertex disks. The curvesf​(γj)f(\gamma_{j})andγj\gamma_{j}become
finite systems of properly embedded arcs and closed curves in the cut
surface. Their boundary endpoints are allowed to slide along the
boundary copies of the previously straightened curves. Corresponding
components are isotopic, and pairwise minimality excludes a bigon with
any boundary copy. The relative bigon criterion therefore gives an
isotopy in the cut surface taking the first system to the second.
Isotopy extension and regluing realize this isotopy while preserving
the earlier curves setwise. Induction overjj, followed by a local
adjustment in the vertex disks, gives an isotopy offfafter whichf​(Γ)=Γf(\Gamma)=\Gamma

with every curve label preserved. Thus every element of the labelled
stabilizer has a representative preserving the chosen finite cell
structure.

Such a representative induces an automorphism of the finite labelled
ribbon graphΓ\Gamma, preserving the cyclic orders at vertices.
The group of these graph automorphisms is finite. We show that the
kernel of the resulting homomorphism from the labelled stabilizer is
trivial. If the induced graph automorphism is the identity, isotope
the representative first to fix every graph vertex, then to fix every
graph edge pointwise. Orientation preservation then allows a further
isotopy making it the identity on a regular neighborhood ofΓ\Gamma.
On every complementary disk it is a boundary-fixing homeomorphism, so
the Alexander trick gives an isotopy relative to the disk boundary to
the identity. These isotopies agree with the identity on the regular
neighborhood and therefore glue globally. Hence the labelled
stabilizer injects into a finite graph-automorphism group and is
finite.
∎

We next cut along a nonseparating curve. The boundary convention is
part of the statements and will also control the regluing ambiguity.

## Convention 12.4(Boundary and spanning-arc conventions).

LetS=Σh,2S=\Sigma_{h,2}

with boundary componentsB+B_{+}andB−B_{-}. All homeomorphisms are
orientation preserving and preserve∂S\partial Ssetwise. They may
exchangeB+B_{+}andB−B_{-}unless the contrary is stated. Isotopies
are through orientation-preserving homeomorphisms preserving the
boundary setwise; the boundary is not fixed pointwise.

Aspanning arcis an essential properly embedded arc joiningB+B_{+}toB−B_{-}. Its endpoints may move along the boundary during
isotopy. Let𝒳​(S)\mathcal{X}(S)denote the set of these isotopy classes.
Under this boundary-setwise convention, a twist supported in a collar
of one boundary component is isotopic to the identity by rotating that
boundary component during the isotopy.

## Lemma 12.5(Faithfulness of the spanning-arc action).

LetS=Σh,2,h≥2.S=\Sigma_{h,2},\qquad h\geq 2.

The orientation-preserving boundary-setwise mapping class group ofSS, in which the two boundary components may be exchanged, acts
faithfully on𝒳​(S)\mathcal{X}(S).

## Proof.

Setn=2​h+1≥5.n=2h+1\geq 5.

Consider a ribbon graph with two verticesv+,v−v_{+},v_{-}and edgese0,e1,…,en−1,e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1},

each joiningv+v_{+}tov−v_{-}. Atv+v_{+}, the cyclic successor of
edgeiiisi+1(modn)i+1\pmod{n}; atv−v_{-}, it isi−2(modn)i-2\pmod{n}. Denote the two half-edges ofeie_{i}by+i+iand−i-i, and letτ\tauinterchange the two half-edges of each edge.
Ifσ\sigmais the cyclic-successor permutation andβ=σ​τ\beta=\sigma\tauis the boundary permutation, thenβ2​(+i)=+(i−1).\beta^{2}(+i)=+(i-1).

The permutationi↦i−1i\mapsto i-1is annn-cycle, andβ\betaalternates the two signs. Henceβ\betais one cycle of length2​n2n. The ribbon graph therefore has one boundary cycle. Its
regular neighborhood hasχ=V−E=2−(2​h+1)=1−2​h\chi=V-E=2-(2h+1)=1-2h

and one boundary component, so it has genushh. Capping that
boundary component gives a cellular embedding in the closed genus-hhsurface with one face.

Remove small open disks aboutv+v_{+}andv−v_{-}. The truncated edge
cores are pairwise disjoint spanning arcsα0,…,αn−1\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1}

inSS, and the one-face calculation says that cutting along all of
them leaves a disk. They are pairwise nonisotopic when endpoints may
move: if two were parallel, two adjacent members of a parallelism class
would cobound a rectangle inSS, which would become a two-edge face
after the vertex disks were restored. This contradicts the existence
of a single face of boundary length2​n>22n>2.

Letf:S→Sf:S\to Sact trivially on𝒳​(S)\mathcal{X}(S). In particular,
it fixes every class[αi][\alpha_{i}]. Because these arcs are pairwise
disjoint and pairwise nonisotopic, an outermost-bigon argument followed
by isotopy extension realizes the individual isotopies simultaneously.
After isotopy,ffpreserves everyαi\alpha_{i}setwise.

The homeomorphismffcannot exchangeB+B_{+}andB−B_{-}. If it
did, collapsing the two boundary components tov+v_{+}andv−v_{-}would give an orientation-preserving ribbon isomorphism that fixes each
labelled edgeeie_{i}and carries the cyclic order atv+v_{+}to the
cyclic order atv−v_{-}. Edgewise fixation would then requirei+1≡i−2(modn)i+1\equiv i-2\pmod{n}

for everyii, sonnwould divide33. This is impossible forn≥5n\geq 5.

ThusffpreservesB+B_{+}andB−B_{-}individually. Orient eachαi\alpha_{i}fromB+B_{+}toB−B_{-}. Sinceffpreserves the
surface orientation and the two boundary components, it preserves the
orientation and the two local sides of everyαi\alpha_{i}. Isotope it
to fix the arcs pointwise and then to fix each boundary interval
between consecutive arc endpoints pointwise. Cutting along the arcs
produces a disk whose induced homeomorphism fixes the entire boundary.
The Alexander trick gives an isotopy relative to that boundary to the
identity. Regluing proves thatffis isotopic to the identity under
the boundary-setwise convention. Therefore the action on𝒳​(S)\mathcal{X}(S)is faithful.
∎

## Lemma 12.6(A finite detecting family of spanning arcs).

LetS=Σh,2,h≥2.S=\Sigma_{h,2},\qquad h\geq 2.

There is a finite family𝒜⊂𝒳​(S)\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}(S)whose pointwise
stabilizer in the orientation-preserving boundary-setwise mapping
class group is trivial. In particular, no mapping class exchanging
the two boundary components fixes every member of𝒜\mathcal{A}.

## Proof.

Choose the pairwise disjoint spanning-arc family𝒜0\mathcal{A}_{0}from the
proof ofSection12; it cutsSSinto a disk.
The arcs together withB+∪B−B_{+}\cup B_{-}form a finite labelled ribbon
graph with disk complement. By the relative bigon criterion, isotopy
extension, and induction over the arcs, every element of the pointwise
stabilizer of𝒜0\mathcal{A}_{0}has a representative preserving this labelled
ribbon graph. As in the proof ofSection12, the resulting homomorphism to the
finite automorphism group of the graph is injective: a trivial graph
automorphism can be fixed on a regular neighborhood and then killed on
the disk complement by the Alexander trick. Boundary-collar twists
add no kernel because they are trivial under the boundary-setwise
convention. Thus the pointwise stabilizerℋ0\mathcal{H}_{0}is finite.

Ifℋ0=1\mathcal{H}_{0}=1, take𝒜=𝒜0\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}. Otherwise choose1≠ϕ∈ℋ01\neq\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{0}. BySection12, somea1∈𝒳​(S)a_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(S)satisfiesϕ​(a1)≠a1\phi(a_{1})\neq a_{1}. Appendinga1a_{1}strictly decreases the
pointwise stabilizer. Iterating yields a strictly descending chain of
subgroups of the finite groupℋ0\mathcal{H}_{0}, hence terminates with
trivial stabilizer. Only𝒜0\mathcal{A}_{0}need be pairwise disjoint.

## Lemma 12.7(Curves intersecting a fixed curve once and spanning arcs).

Letc⊂Σgc\subset\Sigma_{g}be nonseparating and putS=Σg∖int⁡ν​c≅Σg−1,2.S=\Sigma_{g}\setminus\operatorname{int}\nu c\cong\Sigma_{g-1,2}.

Cutting at the unique intersection withccdefines a well-defined,
surjective map on unoriented isotopy classesκc:{[e]:e​is an essential simple closed curve and​i​(c,e)=1}⟶𝒳​(S).\kappa_{c}:\{[e]:e\text{ is an essential simple closed curve and }i(c,e)=1\}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}(S).

The map is equivariant for the unoriented stabilizer ofcc. If a
representative reverses the two sides ofcc, its induced
homeomorphism ofSSexchanges the two boundary components.

## Proof.

Choose representatives ofccandeein minimal position. They
meet in one point, and cuttingeethere gives a proper arc joining
the two boundary components ofSS. Such an arc is essential,
because an arc isotopic into the boundary cannot have endpoints on two
different boundary components.

The resulting class does not depend on the chosen minimal
representative ofee. Lete0e_{0}ande1e_{1}be isotopic
representatives, each meetingcconce, and choose a generic isotopyete_{t}between them. Except at finitely many tangencies,ete_{t}is
transverse tocc; at each tangency, a cancelling pair of
intersection points is created or removed.

Whenever an intermediate curve has more than one intersection withcc, the bigon criterion supplies an innermost bigon between that
curve andcc. A local modification of the isotopy across this
bigon removes the corresponding pair of tangencies from the
one-parameter family. Repeating this finitely many times gives an
isotopy frome0e_{0}toe1e_{1}through curves meetingccexactly
once. Cutting this isotopy alongccgives an isotopy of the
resulting spanning arcs in which the endpoints may move along the
boundary. Reversing the orientation of either
closed curve does not change the resulting unoriented arc class, soκc\kappa_{c}is well defined on the displayed domain.

Conversely, letaabe a spanning arc inSS. In the annulusν​c\nu cused to reglue the two boundary components, join the endpoints
ofaaby an embedded spanning arc meeting the corecconce.
After smoothing the two corners, the union is a simple closed curveeae_{a}withi​(c,ea)=1i(c,e_{a})=1andκc​([ea])=[a]\kappa_{c}([e_{a}])=[a]. Thusκc\kappa_{c}is surjective.

Finally, a mapping class in the unoriented stabilizer ofcchas a
representative preservingccsetwise. Cutting commutes with that
representative, soκc\kappa_{c}is equivariant. Reversal of the two
sides ofccbecomes exchange of the two boundary components ofSS. The map is not asserted to be injective: curves related by
powers ofTcT_{c}can cut to the same spanning-arc class because arc
endpoints are free to move along the boundary.
∎

## Remark 12.10.

The tuple positions are the labels and prescribe the surgery order;
(72) supplies the inductive intersection
hypothesis. No intrinsic order is asserted for arbitrary rim surgeries.

The final section chooses knots realizing the prescribed Alexander

## 15.Alexander modules, knot choices, and homology

This section computes the first Alexander module, chooses the knots
needed for the construction, proves the main results, and records the
group homology and the singular homology of the actual exterior.

## 15.2.One-step Alexander-module calculations

The two attachments have the same graph-of-spaces setup but different
lifted edge spaces.

## Proposition 15.2(One-step Alexander-module calculations).

Let every knot meridian and the distinguished surface meridian map to1∈ℤ1\in\mathbb{Z}.
- (i)

SupposeGD=G∗⟨μ⟩K,G^{D}=G*_{\langle\mu\rangle}K,

where the two meridians are identified. Then𝒜​(GD)≅𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K).\mathcal{A}(G^{D})\cong\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K).
- (ii)

SupposeG′=G∗⟨μ,h⟩(K×⟨z⟩),z=h,G^{\prime}=G*_{\langle\mu,h\rangle}\bigl(K\times\langle z\rangle\bigr),\qquad z=h,

whereϕ​(μ)=1,ϕ​(h)=0.\phi(\mu)=1,\qquad\phi(h)=0.

Then𝒜​(G′)≅𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K).\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K).

In both cases the displayed decomposition is the one supplied by the
chosen graph-of-spaces decomposition; no intrinsic decomposition of the
resulting module into surgery-indexed summands is asserted.

## Proof.

Choose classifying spaces for the vertex and edge groups and join them
by mapping cylinders for the injective edge maps. The universal cover
is a tree of contractible spaces, so the resulting graph of spaces is a
classifying space for the amalgam. The meridional homomorphism is
surjective on every vertex and edge group; hence all corresponding
meridional covers are connected. The lifted graph of spaces has the
same one-edge underlying graph and computesH1​(ker⁡ϕ;ℤ)H_{1}(\ker\phi;\mathbb{Z}). PutQ=Λ/(t−1).Q=\Lambda/(t-1).

Their zeroth homology isQQ, and in both cases the degree-zero
difference map isQ⟶Q⊕Q,q⟼(q,−q),Q\longrightarrow Q\oplus Q,\qquad q\longmapsto(q,-q),

which is injective.

For (i), the restriction to the edge group⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangleis an isomorphism ontoℤ\mathbb{Z}. The lifted edge
space is therefore its universal coverℝ\mathbb{R}, so its first
homology vanishes. The Mayer–Vietoris segment0⟶𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K)⟶𝒜​(GD)⟶Q⟶Q⊕Q0\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(G^{D})\longrightarrow Q\longrightarrow Q\oplus Q

and the preceding injectivity give𝒜​(GD)≅𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K)\mathcal{A}(G^{D})\cong\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K).

For (ii), the lifted edge and product-vertex spaces satisfyA~=ℝ×Sh1,H1​(A~;ℤ)=Q​⟨[h]⟩,\widetilde{A}=\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}_{h},\qquad H_{1}(\widetilde{A};\mathbb{Z})=Q\langle[h]\rangle,

and, by Künneth,H1​(B~;ℤ)≅𝒜​(K)⊕Q​⟨[z]⟩.H_{1}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathcal{A}(K)\oplus Q\langle[z]\rangle.

Thus the degree-one difference map isδ1:Q​⟨[h]⟩⟶𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K)⊕Q​⟨[z]⟩,δ1​(q​[h])=(q​[h],0,−q​[z]).\delta_{1}:Q\langle[h]\rangle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K)\oplus Q\langle[z]\rangle,\qquad\delta_{1}(q[h])=(q[h],0,-q[z]).

This is well defined because[μ,h]=1[\mu,h]=1gives(t−1)​[h]=0(t-1)[h]=0in𝒜​(G)\mathcal{A}(G). Since the edge map identifieshhwith
the positively oriented external circlezz, theQ​⟨[z]⟩Q\langle[z]\rangle-component is multiplication by−1-1and hence an
isomorphism. Thusδ1\delta_{1}is injective; the degree-zero injectivity
then identifies𝒜​(G′)\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime})with its cokernel. The map𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K)⊕Q⟶𝒜​(G)⊕𝒜​(K),(a,k,q)⟼(a+q​[h],k),\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K)\oplus Q\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(G)\oplus\mathcal{A}(K),\qquad(a,k,q)\longmapsto(a+q[h],k),

is surjective and has kernel{(−q​[h],0,q):q∈Q}=im⁡δ1\{(-q[h],0,q):q\in Q\}=\operatorname{im}\delta_{1}. It therefore
induces the required isomorphism. The old class[h][h]is absorbed by
the external-circle summand; it is not killed and imposes no quotient
on𝒜​(G)\mathcal{A}(G).
∎

## 15.3.The total Alexander module

Letϵg={1,g​even,2,g​odd.\epsilon_{g}=\begin{cases}1,&g\text{ even},\\

## Corollary 15.3(Total Alexander module).

For the final surfaceFN⊂S4F_{N}\subset S^{4},𝒜1​(FN)≅⨁j=1g𝒜​(JjD)⊕⨁ν=1ϵg𝒜​(JνV)⊕⨁i=1N𝒜​(Ji).\mathcal{A}_{1}(F_{N})\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{g}\mathcal{A}(J_{j}^{D})\oplus\bigoplus_{\nu=1}^{\epsilon_{g}}\mathcal{A}(J_{\nu}^{V})\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{A}(J_{i}).

In particular,𝒜1​(FN)\mathcal{A}_{1}(F_{N})is a finitely generated torsionΛ\Lambda-module.

## Proof.

For the standard unknotted surface, the meridional abelianization⟨μ⟩→ℤ\langle\mu\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}is an isomorphism, so the associated cover
has trivial fundamental group and zero Alexander module. Theggpreliminary stages useSection15.2(i),
while theϵg\epsilon_{g}vertical-boundary stages and theNNordered
stages useSection15.2(ii). Iteration,
together withSection15.1, gives the displayed direct
sum. Each knot Alexander module has a finite square presentationt​V−VTtV-V^{T}with nonzero determinant, so it is a finitely generated
torsionΛ\Lambda-module; the same holds for the finite direct sum.
∎

where≐\doteqdenotes equality up to a unit±tk\pm t^{k}. The zero
module has polynomial11.

For a knot with Seifert matrixVV, the zeroth Fitting ideal of its

## 15.4.Hyperbolic knots with the required Alexander data

We need one hyperbolic knot with the prescribed module and infinitely
many mutually distinguishable hyperbolic knots with zero module.

## Proposition 15.5(Hyperbolic Alexander-module supplies).
- (i)

For every classical knotKK, there is a nontrivial hyperbolic
knotK♯K^{\sharp}such that𝒜​(K♯)≅𝒜​(K)\mathcal{A}(K^{\sharp})\cong\mathcal{A}(K)

asΛ\Lambda-modules.

There is a sequence of hyperbolic knotsLrL_{r}such that𝒜​(Lr)=0\mathcal{A}(L_{r})=0

for everyrr, and the underlying knot groups, and hence the
meridian-marked knot groups, are pairwise nonisomorphic.

For (ii), Kalfagianni[10, Theorem 1.2]supplies hyperbolic knots withΔ​(t)=1\Delta(t)=1and arbitrarily large volume. Choose them with strictly
increasing volumes. IfVVis a Seifert matrix, the presentation

makes isomorphic finite-volume hyperbolic knot groups have equal
volume; hence the underlying groups, and therefore the
meridian-marked groups, are pairwise nonisomorphic.

For (i), the zero-module case follows from (ii).
Otherwise letVVbe a Seifert matrix forKK. Friedl[5, Theorem 1.1]supplies a hyperbolic knot with a
Seifert matrixSSthat isSS-equivalent toVV. A unimodular
congruence changest​V−VTtV-V^{T}by invertible row and column operations.
For the two elementary enlargementsV1=(V00vT00010),V2=(Vv0001000),V_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}V&0&0\\
v^{T}&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad V_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}V&v&0\\

their Alexander presentation matrices aret​V1−V1T=(t​V−VT−v0t​vT0−10t0),t​V2−V2T=(t​V−VTt​v0−vT0t0−10).tV_{1}-V_{1}^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}tV-V^{T}&-v&0\\
tv^{T}&0&-1\\

ForV1V_{1}, addt−1​vit^{-1}v_{i}times the last row to theii-th old row
and thent​vitv_{i}times the last column to theii-th old column; forV2V_{2}, uset​vitv_{i}times the last row and thent−1​vit^{-1}v_{i}times the
last column. These operations give, respectively,(t​V−VT)⊕(0−1t0),(t​V−VT)⊕(0t−10).(tV-V^{T})\oplus\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
t&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad(tV-V^{T})\oplus\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\

Both displayed2×22\times 2blocks are invertible. Elementary

SinceN≥1N\geq 1, fix an ordered rank-two positioni0i_{0}. Keep all other
knots fixed and letJi0J_{i_{0}}vary through unused members of(Lr)(L_{r}),

## 15.7.Group homology

This is ordinary group homology of classifying spaces, not the
Alexander module of a meridional cover.

## Proposition 15.6(Group homology of the exterior groups).

For the preliminary groupA0=K1D∗⟨μ⟩⋯∗⟨μ⟩KgD,A_{0}=K_{1}^{D}*_{\langle\mu\rangle}\cdots*_{\langle\mu\rangle}K_{g}^{D},

one hasH1​(A0;ℤ)≅ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩,H2​(A0;ℤ)=0.H_{1}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle,\qquad H_{2}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})=0.

For every0≤i≤N0\leq i\leq N,H1​(Gi;ℤ)≅ℤ​⟨[μ]⟩,H2​(Gi;ℤ)=0.H_{1}(G_{i};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\rangle,\qquad H_{2}(G_{i};\mathbb{Z})=0.(78)

Moreover, ifρ:π→A0\rho:\pi\to A_{0}is the push-off homomorphism after the preliminary
surgeries, thenρ​(π)⊂[A0,A0],\rho(\pi)\subset[A_{0},A_{0}],

andρi​(π)⊂[Gi,Gi](0≤i≤N).\rho_{i}(\pi)\subset[G_{i},G_{i}]\qquad(0\leq i\leq N).(79)

## Proof.

The first-homology and commutator-subgroup assertions are exactlySection5.4; it remains to prove the twoH2H_{2}claims.
Every hyperbolic knot exterior is aspherical and has the homology ofS1S^{1}, so for each knot groupKKused here,H1​(K;ℤ)=ℤ​⟨[m]⟩,H2​(K;ℤ)=0.H_{1}(K;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle[m]\rangle,\qquad H_{2}(K;\mathbb{Z})=0.

ForA0A_{0}, use the star-shaped graph of groups with central vertex⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangle, leaf groupsKjDK_{j}^{D}, and edge groups⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\rangle. The central and leaf vertex groups have
vanishing second homology, so the degree-two part of Mayer–Vietoris is0⟶H2​(A0;ℤ)⟶ℤg→δ1ℤ⊕ℤg,0\longrightarrow H_{2}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{g}\xrightarrow{\delta_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{g},

whereδ1​(n1,…,ng)=(−∑jnj,n1,…,ng).\delta_{1}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{g})=\bigl(-\textstyle\sum_{j}n_{j},n_{1},\ldots,n_{g}\bigr).

The leaf coordinates makeδ1\delta_{1}injective, and henceH2​(A0;ℤ)=0H_{2}(A_{0};\mathbb{Z})=0.

Now consider one rank-two attachmentG′=G∗AB,A=⟨μ,h⟩≅ℤ2,B=K×⟨z⟩,z=h.G^{\prime}=G*_{A}B,\qquad A=\langle\mu,h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},\qquad B=K\times\langle z\rangle,\qquad z=h.

At every such stage,[h]=0∈H1​(G;ℤ)[h]=0\in H_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})

bySection5.4. Hence the degree-one edge map is[μ]⟼([μ],−[m]),[h]⟼(0,−[z]),[\mu]\longmapsto([\mu],-[m]),\qquad[h]\longmapsto(0,-[z]),

and is injective because[m],[z][m],[z]form a basis ofH1​(B;ℤ)H_{1}(B;\mathbb{Z}).
Moreover,H2​(B;ℤ)≅H1​(K;ℤ)⊗H1​(⟨z⟩;ℤ)≅ℤ​⟨[m]∧[z]⟩,H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{1}(K;\mathbb{Z})\otimes H_{1}(\langle z\rangle;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\langle[m]\wedge[z]\rangle,

whileH2​(A;ℤ)≅ℤ​⟨[μ]∧[h]⟩.H_{2}(A;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\langle[\mu]\wedge[h]\rangle.

TheH2​(B)H_{2}(B)-component ofδ2:H2​(A;ℤ)⟶H2​(G;ℤ)⊕H2​(B;ℤ)\delta_{2}:H_{2}(A;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{2}(G;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z})

sends the displayed generator isomorphically, up to sign, to[m]∧[z][m]\wedge[z]. In the exact segmentH2​(A)→δ2H2​(G)⊕H2​(B)⟶H2​(G′)⟶H1​(A)→δ1H1​(G)⊕H1​(B),H_{2}(A)\xrightarrow{\delta_{2}}H_{2}(G)\oplus H_{2}(B)\longrightarrow H_{2}(G^{\prime})\longrightarrow H_{1}(A)\xrightarrow{\delta_{1}}H_{1}(G)\oplus H_{1}(B),

injectivity ofδ1\delta_{1}givesH2​(G′;ℤ)≅coker⁡δ2≅H2​(G;ℤ).H_{2}(G^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{coker}\delta_{2}\cong H_{2}(G;\mathbb{Z}).

Starting fromA0A_{0}and applying this at the vertical-boundary and then

## 15.8.Homology of the actual exterior

The singular homology of the four-dimensional exterior is different
from the preceding group homology.

## Proposition 15.7(Homology of the surface exterior).

LetF=FN⊂S4F=F_{N}\subset S^{4}be any final genus-ggsurface in the
construction, and putEF=S4∖int⁡ν​F.E_{F}=S^{4}\setminus\operatorname{int}\nu F.

ThenH0​(EF;ℤ)=ℤ,H1​(EF;ℤ)=ℤ,H2​(EF;ℤ)=ℤ2​g,H_{0}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z},\qquad H_{1}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z},\qquad H_{2}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{2g},H3​(EF;ℤ)=0,H4​(EF;ℤ)=0.H_{3}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})=0,\qquad H_{4}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})=0.

Consequentlyχ​(EF)=2​g.\chi(E_{F})=2g.

These statements are compatible withH2​(π1​(EF);ℤ)=0H_{2}(\pi_{1}(E_{F});\mathbb{Z})=0fromSection15.7.

## Proof.

Radial projection in the punctured normal disk fibers, extended by the
identity outside the normal neighborhood, givesS4∖F≃EF.S^{4}\setminus F\simeq E_{F}.

Alexander duality givesH~i​(S4∖F;ℤ)≅H~3−i​(F;ℤ).\widetilde{H}_{i}(S^{4}\setminus F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\widetilde{H}^{3-i}(F;\mathbb{Z}).

For a connected closed oriented genus-ggsurface this yieldsH~0=0,H1≅H2​(F;ℤ)≅ℤ,H2≅H1​(F;ℤ)≅ℤ2​g,H3≅H~0​(F;ℤ)=0.\widetilde{H}_{0}=0,\qquad H_{1}\cong H^{2}(F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z},\qquad H_{2}\cong H^{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2g},\qquad H_{3}\cong\widetilde{H}^{0}(F;\mathbb{Z})=0.

SinceEFE_{F}is a compact connected44-manifold with nonempty
boundary,H4​(EF;ℤ)=0H_{4}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})=0, and the displayed groups giveχ​(EF)=1−1+2​g=2​g\chi(E_{F})=1-1+2g=2g.

WritingGF=π1​(EF)G_{F}=\pi_{1}(E_{F}), the groupH2​(GF;ℤ)H_{2}(G_{F};\mathbb{Z})is the homology of
a classifying space, whereasH2​(EF;ℤ)H_{2}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})is singular homology of
the actual exterior; no asphericity ofEFE_{F}is asserted. The Hopf
exact sequenceπ2​(EF)⟶H2​(EF;ℤ)⟶H2​(GF;ℤ)⟶0\pi_{2}(E_{F})\longrightarrow H_{2}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{2}(G_{F};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow 0

therefore explains the compatibility ofH2​(EF;ℤ)≅ℤ2​gH_{2}(E_{F};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2g}withH2​(GF;ℤ)=0H_{2}(G_{F};\mathbb{Z})=0.
∎
